	<item>
		<title>SageMaker Inference now supports Multi-Instance GPU to maximize GPU utilization for model endpoints</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/sagemaker-inference-now-supports-multi-instance-gpu-to-maximize-gpu-utilization-for-model-endpoints/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Shantanu Tripathi]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 06 Nov 2026 18:01:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">45834970ac5a8ce39a39de22bbc2ae33f7618139</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker Inference now supports GPU partitioning with NVIDIA Multi-Instance GPU (MIG), which divides one GPU into hardware-isolated partitions so you can run a different model in each or take a right-sized fraction of a newer accelerator. This post shows how two optional API fields place one model per partition, and what we measured against a whole older GPU.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;When you deploy a model to a GPU endpoint, the smallest unit of GPU you can request is one whole accelerator.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This constraint can cost you twice. First, you can’t host more models on an instance than it has accelerators. An &lt;code&gt;ml.p4d.24xlarge&lt;/code&gt; has eight A100s, so it holds eight models, whether each one needs 40 GB or 14 GB. Second, and less obvious, it makes every deployment come down to a choice of which full GPU to deploy to. As your concurrency and prompt lengths grow, a full GPU on an older instance stops being enough, but a full GPU on a newer one is more than your model can use. You end up either under-served or paying for capability you never reach.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, Amazon SageMaker Inference supports GPU partitioning, which uses &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/tesla/mig-user-guide/latest/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVIDIA Multi-Instance GPU&lt;/a&gt; (MIG) to divide a physical GPU into independent partitions. Each partition receives a fixed share of the GPU’s compute units and a dedicated block of its memory, with isolation enforced in hardware. That changes both constraints: you can place a different model in each partition, and you can choose a fraction of a newer accelerator instead of a whole one of either generation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post covers both. The first part is the direct benefit: deploy more models than you have GPUs, with isolation between them. The second is the one customers ask about when the question turns to cost: how the granularity of partitioning helps you pick the right-sized unit, and what we measured when we did.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One thing to know before you read further: GPU partitioning works on endpoints that use &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reduce-model-deployment-costs-by-50-on-average-using-sagemakers-latest-features/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;inference components&lt;/a&gt;, the construct you use to deploy several models to one endpoint and set the compute each model gets. Partitioning builds directly on it. A partition is a resource a component asks for, in the same place it already asks for CPU and memory. If you haven’t used inference components, this &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reduce-model-deployment-costs-by-50-on-average-using-sagemakers-latest-features/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;post&lt;/a&gt; introduces them. Single-model endpoints, serverless endpoints, and heterogeneous instance pools cannot be partitioned.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-customers-do-today-and-why-each-option-falls-short"&gt;What customers do today, and why each option falls short&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you have more models than accelerators, you have had three choices.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Run fewer, larger endpoints and batch harder – &lt;/strong&gt;This raises utilization, but only when the models are interchangeable. A portfolio of fine-tuned variants or per-tenant models is not.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use multi-model endpoints –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;These load and unload models from a shared GPU on demand, which fits sparse, bursty traffic. But models share the device without isolation, so a heavy request for one model affects the others. A model that isn’t loaded also pays a load penalty on first use.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Move to self-managed Kubernetes –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;This is where customers who need real GPU partitioning have gone. MIG works there, but you take on driver versioning, partition configuration, a device plugin, scheduling, and keeping it all consistent across a fleet. That’s a lot of work for a hardware capability your instances already have.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-mig"&gt;Why MIG&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;NVIDIA Multi-Instance GPU (MIG) splits one GPU into as many as seven instances, depending on the GPU. Each instance has its own compute, cache, memory and memory bandwidth. Because the split is in hardware rather than software, you get guarantees that other sharing methods can’t give:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Predictable latency –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Each partition has dedicated memory bandwidth, so one model’s traffic spike doesn’t show up in another model’s p99.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fault isolation –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;A crash or memory error stays inside its partition instead of taking down every model on the GPU.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Right-sized capacity –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Small and mid-sized models get a slice that fits them instead of a whole GPU. You can mix slice sizes on one device.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No cold starts –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Every model keeps its own partition and stays loaded, with no load penalty and no eviction.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tenant isolation –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Memory protection between partitions is enforced in hardware, which makes security and compliance reviews simpler.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reliable planning and chargeback –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;A partition performs the same way regardless of its neighbors, so load tests predict production. GPU cost also maps cleanly to a model, team or tenant.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Partitioning has boundaries worth knowing up front. The maximum number of partitions depends on the accelerator and never exceeds seven. MIG itself only allows a layout to change while its partitions are idle, and on SageMaker AI that means choosing a layout when you create the endpoint. A model that needs more than one GPU still needs whole devices. Within those boundaries, you get the isolation of separate GPUs without dedicating one to each model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="partitioning-without-the-platform-work"&gt;Partitioning without the platform work&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Until now, getting MIG meant running the Kubernetes stack yourself. GPU partitioning on Amazon SageMaker AI removes that step: you pick a partition profile for your model, and SageMaker AI handles GPU configuration, placement and scaling. You get hardware-isolated GPU sharing on a managed endpoint, without having to run the platform underneath it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Salesforce has been part of this work from the start, helping define the requirements and validating the initial implementation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“NVIDIA MIG partitions a single GPU into multiple isolated instances, each with dedicated compute and memory resources. Salesforce sees this as an important capability because many inference workloads do not require an entire GPU. MIG would allow us to consolidate workloads safely, significantly improve GPU utilization, reduce inference costs, and maintain predictable performance and strong isolation.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This capability would help us scale AI Inference more efficiently across different use cases. Salesforce has helped define requirements, validated the initial solution. We are now preparing to validate the solution with representative production workload and demonstrate strong customer demand with AWS. We believe MIG support would deliver meaningful technical and business value for enterprise customers.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Richard Chang, Software Engineering Architect, Salesforce&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPU partitioning adds two optional fields to API calls you already make. There are no new API operations, no new resource types, and no new AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) actions, and an endpoint that omits both fields behaves exactly as it does today.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;AcceleratorPartitionLayouts&lt;/code&gt; goes on a production variant in &lt;code&gt;CreateEndpointConfig&lt;/code&gt; and declares how each accelerator is divided:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "EndpointConfigName": "multi-model-partitioned",
  "ExecutionRoleArn": "&amp;lt;execution-role-arn&amp;gt;",
  "ProductionVariants": [
    {
      "VariantName": "AllTraffic",
      "InstanceType": "ml.p4d.24xlarge",
      "InitialInstanceCount": 1,
      "AcceleratorPartitionLayouts": [
        { "AcceleratorPartitionTypes": ["mig-4g.20gb", "mig-2g.10gb", "mig-1g.5gb"] }
      ]
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An A100 has seven compute slices to divide. The preceding layout uses all seven, so each of the eight A100s on the p4d instance carries three partitions and the endpoint provides 24, eight of each type.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The carve applies uniformly. One layout entry describes how one accelerator is divided, and every accelerator on every instance in the variant is divided the same way. The list isn’t positional, so an entry doesn’t map to a specific GPU.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;AcceleratorPartitionType&lt;/code&gt; goes on an inference component and selects which partition the model runs on:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "InferenceComponentName": "summarizer",
  "EndpointName": "multi-model-partitioned",
  "VariantName": "AllTraffic",
  "Specification": {
    "ModelName": "summarizer-model",
    "ComputeResourceRequirements": {
      "AcceleratorPartitionType": "mig-4g.20gb",
      "NumberOfAcceleratorDevicesRequired": 1,
      "NumberOfCpuCoresRequired": 1,
      "MinMemoryRequiredInMb": 2048
    }
  },
  "RuntimeConfig": { "CopyCount": 1 }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each component copy occupies one partition of the requested type. With eight partitions of each type on the instance, raising &lt;code&gt;CopyCount&lt;/code&gt; places additional copies on free partitions. This is useful for a small model serving high concurrency, where a single process leaves the GPU waiting on requests.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;NumberOfAcceleratorDevicesRequired&lt;/code&gt; is always 1 here. With a partition type set it means one partition rather than one accelerator. &lt;code&gt;NumberOfCpuCoresRequired&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;MinMemoryRequiredInMb&lt;/code&gt; still describe host CPU and host memory, because GPU memory comes from the partition type.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Invocation doesn’t change. &lt;code&gt;InvokeEndpoint&lt;/code&gt; addresses a model by inference component name, and the partition it occupies never appears in the request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Partitioning happens while the endpoint is provisioned. When it reaches &lt;code&gt;InService&lt;/code&gt; every accelerator is carved and the partitions are empty, which is a valid state as components land on them afterward. In our testing, carving accelerators added roughly 17 seconds to provisioning. The rest of the time is the usual instance launch and container pull.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="partition-profiles-by-gpu"&gt;Partition profiles by GPU&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The profiles available to you are a property of the GPU, so start by finding out which GPU your instance type carries and look it up in &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/tesla/mig-user-guide/latest/supported-mig-profiles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVIDIA’s supported MIG profiles&lt;/a&gt;, which is the authority on the full profile set and the combinations valid on one device.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Instance family&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Accelerator&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;GPU memory&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Granularity (compute / memory)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Available partition profiles&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Max partitions per GPU&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;P4d&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;NVIDIA A100&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;40 GB&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;7 / 8&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;mig-1g.5gb, mig-1g.10gb &lt;em&gt;(see driver note)&lt;/em&gt;, mig-2g.10gb, mig-3g.20gb, mig-4g.20gb, mig-7g.40gb&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;7&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;P5&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;NVIDIA H100&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;80 GB&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;7 / 8&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;mig-1g.10gb, mig-1g.20gb, mig-2g.20gb, mig-3g.40gb, mig-4g.40gb, mig-7g.80gb&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;7&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;G7&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;NVIDIA RTX PRO 4500 Blackwell&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;32 GB&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2 / 2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;mig-1g.16gb, mig-2g.32gb&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;G7e&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;96 GB&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;4 / 4&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;mig-1g.24gb, mig-2g.48gb, mig-4g.96gb&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;4&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Read the profile name as compute units followed by GPU memory: &lt;code&gt;mig-3g.40gb&lt;/code&gt; takes three compute units and 40 GB. The granularity column is what limits a layout. An A100 and an H100 divide into sevenths on compute and eighths on memory, while G7 divides into halves and G7e into quarters. Profile names differ between GPUs even where the slice counts match, so don’t carry a layout from one instance family to another without checking it against this table.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Within those limits you have real choice. On a P4d or P5 you can carve seven of the smallest profile, or two 3g partitions, or the 4g plus 2g plus 1g layout used in the earlier example, or take the whole device as one. On a G7e you can take four of the smallest, or two 1g beside a 2g, or two 2g, or one whole device. On a G7, either two partitions or one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The 3g profile is the one to know, because its two dimensions don’t match: it takes three of the seven compute units but four of the eight memory units. Two of them fill the GPU and nothing else will fit beside them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The high-level rule:&lt;/strong&gt; Every profile in your layout must be one your GPU offers, and the compute and memory units your profiles add up to must not exceed what one device provides. Both are checked when you create the endpoint configuration, and either one failing is a rejection rather than a fallback to a whole accelerator.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Driver note for P4d:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;mig-1g.10gb requires an R525 or newer NVIDIA driver, and the default P4d image ships 470.256.02. Request it on the default image and the carve fails without an error naming the profile. Set &lt;code&gt;InferenceAmiVersion&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;al2023-ami-sagemaker-inference-gpu-4-1&lt;/code&gt; on the production variant to get an image on the 580 driver line, which offers it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="choosing-a-layout-for-your-workload"&gt;Choosing a layout for your workload&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Choosing well is a separate question from choosing a layout that is merely accepted. A partition hands you compute and memory in fixed proportions, and the two do different jobs during inference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compute units are what you need for prefill.&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Prefill reads the prompt and builds its representation. This is the compute-heavy phase, and it scales with the streaming multiprocessors, and so the TFLOPs, your partition holds. The longer your inputs and the more prompts arriving at once, the more it governs how quickly you return a first token.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Memory units are what you need for decode and for capacity.&lt;/strong&gt; Generating tokens re-reads the model weights on every step, so decode is bounded by memory bandwidth rather than by math, and memory also caps how much KV cache you can hold. That cap in turn sets the context length you can serve and how many sequences you can serve concurrently.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Almost no real workload exercises only one of these, so the goal isn’t to pick a dimension but to choose a combination that supports your use case.&lt;/strong&gt; Choose a profile whose proportions cover your heaviest phase while leaving the other adequately served. On the compute side, what matters is how long your prompts are and how many arrive at once. On the memory side, it’s your output length, your context window, and your concurrency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Memory is the easier of the two to underestimate, because a partition has to hold three things and not just the model: the weights, which for an 8B model at FP16 are about 16 GB. The KV cache, sized for the context length and the number of concurrent sequences you intend to allow, which is the term people leave out and the one that grows with traffic. And 1–2 GB of runtime overhead for the CUDA context and the serving process. Add those up before choosing a profile, because a partition sized to the weights alone will load the model and then fail under load, or quietly serve a much shorter context than you configured. For a concrete reference point, that same 8B model at FP16 comfortably serves an 8,000-token context on a 40 GB partition.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="where-mig-adds-the-most-value"&gt;Where MIG adds the most value&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A portfolio of small and mid-size models –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The direct case. Fine-tuned variants, embedding models, rerankers, and classifiers that each leave most of an accelerator unused now share one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-tenant model serving –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;When you host a model per customer, MIG gives you a hardware boundary between them: a process in one partition can’t read another partition’s memory or take its throughput. Scope the claim accurately, though. Models on the same instance still share host CPU, system memory, storage, networking, and endpoint routing, so partitioning is an isolation mechanism for the GPU and not a complete tenancy boundary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-stage pipelines on one instance –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;A transcription model, a diarization model, and a language model, or an embedder, a reranker, and a generator. Each stage is a separately addressable component on its own partition, and a network hop between stages disappears.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A small model in the request path –&lt;/strong&gt; A safety classifier or guardrail model that has to run on every request but cannot justify an accelerator, with predictable latency because it isn’t time-slicing against the main model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Challengers and canaries –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Run a candidate model on a small partition beside the model serving production traffic instead of standing up a second endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="performance-a-slice-of-a-newer-gpu-against-a-whole-older-one"&gt;Performance: A slice of a newer GPU against a whole older one&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In addition to bin-packing, the other benefit you get is granularity on high-end GPUs: more GPU options to deploy a model onto, and the choice of which one is yours. Every supported accelerator becomes several sizes rather than one, so the practical way to think about it is that each profile is a GPU option in its own right, sitting alongside the whole accelerators you already choose between. That matters most when you are considering a larger or newer instance type, because moving up stops being all-or-nothing: you can take as much or as little of that bigger GPU as your workload needs, at any of the sizes it publishes, instead of paying for the whole accelerator.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To see whether that pays off, we compared one &lt;code&gt;mig-3g.40gb&lt;/code&gt; partition of an H100 against one whole, uncarved A100-40GB. The two have comparable GPU memory, which makes it a clean way to see how powerful a slice of a more advanced instance can be.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We used &lt;a href="https://github.com/sgl-project/genai-bench" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;genai-bench&lt;/a&gt; to generate load, driving both endpoints from the same client so the GPU is the only variable. Both sides ran Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on production SageMaker real-time endpoints, one model replica each, with an identical container image, tokenizer, and client configuration. Both were invoked through the SageMaker AI native OpenAI-compatible route. We swept five traffic shapes, written D(input tokens, output tokens) and ranging from short prompts to long-context summarization, each across a concurrency ladder from 2 to 512.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-22216-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-22216-1-1.png" alt="Output tokens per second versus concurrency for an H100 mig-3g.40gb partition and a full A100-40GB across five traffic shapes" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Aggregate output tokens per second for one mig-3g.40gb partition of an H100 against one full A100-40GB, across five traffic shapes and a concurrency ladder from 2 to 512&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-22216-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-22216-2-1.png" alt="Cost benefit per million output tokens versus concurrency for five traffic shapes, with the break-even crossover marked" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Partition cost benefit per million output tokens against concurrency for the same five traffic shapes&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Four things stand out.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The partition is faster everywhere –&lt;/strong&gt; It delivered more output tokens per second in every scenario at every concurrency, by 53 percent to 89 percent at the load where each scenario’s cost benefit is largest, and lower inter-token latency in every one too, by 8.9 percent to 42.1 percent. The gap widens with load.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Time to first token is lower in every case, by 5.6 percent to 47.5 percent.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;On cost it’s 18–34 percent cheaper per million output tokens, above a crossover that moves earlier as prompts lengthen.&lt;/strong&gt; Concurrency 64 for 100-token prompts, 4 for 8,000-token prompts. Below the crossover the whole A100 is the cheaper unit. These prices are all on-demand, which at the time of analysis were measured as $25.251286 per hour for an &lt;code&gt;ml.p4d.24xlarge&lt;/code&gt; and $63.296000 per hour for an &lt;code&gt;ml.p5.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; in the US West (Oregon) AWS Region. Purely for our own analysis, we derived the unit cost by dividing the whole-instance cost by the number of units on it: 8 on the p4d, and 16 on the p5, where our partition is half of a GPU.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;At the top of the ladder on long-context traffic, the A100 stopped serving.&lt;/strong&gt; On both the 6,000- and 8,000-token workloads it failed a third to a half of its requests against a serving-path deadline, while the partition failed none. That is why those two panels end earlier than the rest.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Treat this as a case-by-case comparison, not a verdict.&lt;/strong&gt; The useful mental model is to regard the available GPU fractions as GPU types in their own right. List them alongside whole accelerators, work out what your traffic actually demands of compute and of memory, and compare them on your own workload. For high-concurrency, long-input traffic the partition won on both performance and cost here. For short-input or low-concurrency workloads the whole A100 remained cheaper. Partitioning isn’t a one-size-fits-all answer. It gives you more sizes to choose from, so you can pick the one that fits your workload.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-to-know-before-you-commit-to-a-layout"&gt;What to know before you commit to a layout&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Partitioning changes the shape of an endpoint, and a few properties are worth understanding before you design one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A component’s partition type is fixed for the life of that component. Moving a model to a different profile means creating a new component. Changing the endpoint’s carve means a new endpoint and a traffic migration, because updating an endpoint configuration doesn’t re-carve hosts that are already running. This makes partitioning a good fit for a stable portfolio and a poor one for a mix you reshuffle daily.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A requested profile has to exist in the endpoint’s layout. There is no fallback to a whole accelerator, so a component asking for a profile the variant didn’t declare is rejected.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Partitioning applies to real-time inference on inference component endpoints. Serverless endpoints and heterogeneous instance pools aren’t supported, and asynchronous invocation cannot address an inference component, so async workloads are out of scope.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You’re billed for the full GPU instance, as you are today. Partitioning raises how much of that instance you can use. It doesn’t change its hourly price.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Finally, a model that genuinely needs a full accelerator, or more than one, should keep having one. No partition spans accelerators.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="supported-instances-and-regions"&gt;Supported instances and Regions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPU partitioning is available on the P4d, P5, G7, and G7e instance families. Support is at the family level, so smaller sizes within a family are covered.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;It’s available in the following AWS Regions: US East (N. Virginia), US East (Ohio), US West (Oregon), Asia Pacific (Singapore), Asia Pacific (Tokyo), Europe (Frankfurt), and Europe (Ireland).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Instance availability varies by Region, so confirm that your chosen instance type is offered in your Region and that you have endpoint usage quota for it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To partition a GPU endpoint, work out the memory and compute each of your model’s needs, pick the profiles that fit, declare a layout on your endpoint configuration, and deploy each model as an inference component that requests a profile from that layout. Nothing about how you invoke your models changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the API reference, the full profile tables for each instance family, and a step-by-step walkthrough, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/inference-components-accelerator-partitioning.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GPU partitioning&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon SageMaker AI Developer Guide.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To try it yourself, run the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/tree/main/03-features/GPU-partitions" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GPU partitioning sample notebook&lt;/a&gt;, which deploys several models onto partitions of a single GPU endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The broader change is worth keeping in mind as you plan capacity. Every supported accelerator is now several sizes rather than one. Whether a partition of a newer GPU or a whole accelerator of an older one is the right unit for a given model is no longer a question you need to answer by rounding up. It’s one you can measure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Taxonomy of agentic primitives in Amazon Quick</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/taxonomy-of-agentic-primitives-in-amazon-quick/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Thomas Delteil]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 06 Nov 2026 17:34:45 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Suite]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Thought Leadership]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">755c7d8248913b9c1698eceff615a3e12637e5e9</guid>

					<description>As we built Amazon Quick, its agents grew from answering questions to taking actions, and the same primitives kept getting rebuilt under different names. This post shares the taxonomy we converged on — triggers, agents, capabilities, and learned context — found by decomposing agentic work into four plain questions: When, What, Who, and How.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Over the past year, as we built Amazon Quick, our agents went from answering questions to taking actions: scheduling meetings, running research, generating reports. As the system evolved, one problem kept resurfacing: there was no shared definition of the primitives that agentic work is built from. The same few ideas kept getting rebuilt under different names: an actor that does the work, a signal that starts it, a packaged procedure, and a memory that carries lessons forward. This repetition leaked into product design and onto the user.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Take the user who says “&lt;em&gt;monitor my Slack channel and send me a daily digest&lt;/em&gt;”. Is that a new agent, a scheduled instruction to an existing one, a workflow, or a skill? These are different questions, but without a shared vocabulary they collapse into one confusing choice.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post shares the taxonomy we converged on: one set of primitives found by decomposing agentic work into four plain questions: &lt;em&gt;When?&lt;/em&gt;, &lt;em&gt;What?&lt;/em&gt;, &lt;em&gt;Who?&lt;/em&gt;, and &lt;em&gt;How?&lt;/em&gt; Most of the framework stays invisible to end users by design, but a shared vocabulary makes the system easier to reason about for anyone building beneath it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-framework-at-a-glance"&gt;The framework at a glance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/03/ML-21505-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/03/ML-21505-1.png" alt="Triggers (When and What) feed an agent (Who) that uses capabilities (How) along a less-to-more deterministic axis, with a session runtime and background learning" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: How triggers, agents, and capabilities relate, with learning feeding back from the session runtime&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A &lt;strong&gt;trigger&lt;/strong&gt; starts the work and carries the task (&lt;em&gt;When&lt;/em&gt; and &lt;em&gt;What&lt;/em&gt;), an &lt;strong&gt;agent&lt;/strong&gt; takes it on (&lt;em&gt;Who&lt;/em&gt;) and uses its &lt;strong&gt;capabilities&lt;/strong&gt; (tools, skills, and workflows) to perform the task (&lt;em&gt;How&lt;/em&gt;). This post describes these primitives and how they relate to one another. It’s the first post in a series, and upcoming posts cover our agentic operating system, background learning, proactive feeds, and much more.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="triggers-what-causes-work-to-begin"&gt;Triggers: What causes work to begin&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Triggers are the activation mechanism for getting work done, but they also carry instructions: a human message is both the moment of activation and the description of what to do. They are both the &lt;em&gt;When?&lt;/em&gt; and the &lt;em&gt;What?&lt;/em&gt; of a task.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Human-initiated:&lt;/strong&gt; A person opens a conversation and asks the agent to do something. Interactive, synchronous, conversational.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent-delegated:&lt;/strong&gt; One agent, mid-task, invokes another to handle a sub-problem. Programmatic, composable, potentially recursive.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scheduled:&lt;/strong&gt; Time-based activation, like “run financial research every Monday at 9am.” Deterministic, with the same standing instructions applied across runs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Event-driven:&lt;/strong&gt; A signal from an external system, such as a new email, a Slack mention, a file change, or a webhook.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The type of trigger conditions the execution: a scheduled background task might stay conservative (for example, do nothing if uncertain), while a human-initiated one would be responsive (for example, ask rather than stop).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="agents"&gt;Agents&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We define an agent as a &lt;em&gt;persistent, learning actor&lt;/em&gt;. It’s the &lt;em&gt;Who?&lt;/em&gt; in any execution, and the anchor against which learning accrues. Two agents given identical input can differ, because they carry different instructions, capabilities, and histories. The agent isn’t the instructions it follows. It’s the entity that interprets them. It consists of three things:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Instructions:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Standing guidance defining persona, role, context files, and operating principles. A Finance Agent behaves differently from a general assistant primarily because of this.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Capabilities:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The tools, skills, workflows, and sub-agents available for use, updated dynamically. A charting library can be added in one session and removed the next.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Learned context:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Memory that accumulates through use: the queries that work for a tool, the formatting users prefer, the edge cases they care about. This is how the tenth execution beats the first without anyone changing the instructions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Everything else is extrinsic: the large language model (LLM) underlying the agent is a swappable model parameter, conversation history is per-session state, and triggers belong to the platform.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A consequence of this definition is that a user request “&lt;em&gt;I want an agent that monitors my Slack and sends a digest every morning&lt;/em&gt;” is usually a &lt;em&gt;trigger added to an existing agent&lt;/em&gt;, not a new agent or workflow. With this framing, we can merge chat, scheduled, and “autonomous” agents into one actor that you can converse with, schedule, and that can orchestrate and inspect its own past work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="capabilities"&gt;Capabilities&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Capabilities are &lt;em&gt;How&lt;/em&gt; an agent acts, spanning a spectrum from fully agentic to fully deterministic. That axis is really a &lt;em&gt;confidence contract&lt;/em&gt;: how much latitude the agent keeps versus how much the user fixes in advance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tools&lt;/strong&gt; are atomic operations with a typed schema: send an email, query a database, read a file. Whether a local function, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server, or another agent behind a protocol boundary, the caller sees the same interface: name, description, parameters, result.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Skills&lt;/strong&gt; are structured guidance with room for judgment: instructions bundled with resources (templates, scripts, examples) that describe an approach rather than a rigid sequence. They load through &lt;em&gt;progressive disclosure&lt;/em&gt;, so the agent sees only names and short descriptions until one becomes relevant, then reads the full instructions in. A skill can therefore bundle arbitrary depth of expertise without a constant cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Workflows&lt;/strong&gt; are deterministic coordination: declared steps, control flow, branching, and error handling that execute in fixed order, with decision logic in code rather than model inference. Think of a purchase-approval route: budget check, manager sign-off, then finance, each recorded. The route is fixed, but the judgment &lt;em&gt;inside&lt;/em&gt; a step is still the agent’s.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/03/ML-21505-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/03/ML-21505-2.png" alt="A skill lets the agent interpret guidance and choose the order (less deterministic); a workflow fixes the step order in code (more deterministic)" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Skills versus workflows, which differ in whether the agent or the code decides the step order&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-each-primitive-earns-its-keep"&gt;Why skills and workflows outlast model improvements&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A natural question arises: if models keep improving, won’t skills and workflows become unnecessary? We don’t think so, because each holds value independent of model capability. Skills are the unit for capturing, versioning, and sharing institutional expertise, while workflows deliver auditability, step-wise visibility, and deterministic multi-service coordination. Nor are they fixed: a well-understood skill can tighten into a workflow (&lt;em&gt;crystallization&lt;/em&gt;), a workflow that keeps hitting edge cases can loosen back into a skill (&lt;em&gt;dissolution&lt;/em&gt;). This is done in Quick through our continuous learning framework, which we will describe in a future post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-this-means-in-practice"&gt;What this means in practice&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Return to “&lt;em&gt;monitor my Slack channel and send me a daily digest&lt;/em&gt;”. Because these primitives compose, the same request can be met several valid ways:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A scheduled task with a one-line instruction, leaving the agent to figure out the rest.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The same task backed by a skill with explicit formatting rules and channel priorities.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The same task invoking a workflow with deterministic steps and error handling.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All three produce a daily digest on a schedule. What differs is the confidence contract. A user who trusts the agent wants the first, one burned by inconsistency the second, one running a compliance-sensitive process the third.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Learning shifts that trajectory over time. A task that starts as one sentence (“&lt;em&gt;check my email for urgent items&lt;/em&gt;”) accumulates domain-bound learnings: effective queries, preferred formatting, and which senders matter. The tenth run is markedly better, with no one encoding that by hand. The right design is the simplest option that meets the user’s confidence requirement, with a path to graduate through autonomous learning when they need more.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The value of this agentic primitive taxonomy is that four plain questions, &lt;em&gt;When&lt;/em&gt;, &lt;em&gt;What&lt;/em&gt;, &lt;em&gt;Who&lt;/em&gt;, and &lt;em&gt;How&lt;/em&gt;, are enough to place any piece of agentic work, from a one-line chat request to a scheduled, compliance-grade process. Sharing one vocabulary lets builders reason about the system and lets the product hide complexity. Best of all, the user never has to learn any of it. They say what they want, and the system assembles the appropriate primitives to do their job.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Next in the series: the agentic operating system that builds on these primitives and powers Amazon Quick.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="keep-exploring"&gt;Keep exploring&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Ready to try? Start with the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick getting-started guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Serverless MCP server for AI agent data lake access on Amazon ECS Fargate</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/serverless-mcp-server-for-ai-agent-data-lake-access-on-amazon-ecs-fargate/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Aishwarya Pawar]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 06 Nov 2026 17:13:13 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Elastic Container Service]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Fargate]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">64b64349a40669287b5c781082e75c92e2cc9153</guid>

					<description>Learn how to build a serverless Model Context Protocol (MCP) server on Amazon ECS with AWS Fargate that gives AI agents secure, metadata-driven access to an enterprise data lake. Exposing a new dataset requires only a JSON file, with no per-dataset integration code.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Organizations building applications powered by artificial intelligence (AI) face a recurring challenge: giving large language model (LLM) agents access to enterprise data lakes without duplicating integration logic across every agent and application. Most teams begin by wiring agents directly to databases and APIs, with no shared gateway such as a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server in between. Each new dataset demands custom code, and each agent reimplements its own access control. As the number of datasets or agents grows, permission enforcement drifts, and onboarding a new data source becomes a multi-day engineering effort.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our team supports Amazon’s North America Sort Center (NASC) network, where planning teams rely on operational data to manage capacity, labor, and package flow. To give those teams conversational access to that data, we built a serverless &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol (MCP)&lt;/a&gt; server on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS)&lt;/a&gt; with AWS Fargate to address this problem for planning teams in NASC. The server acts as a centralized, agent-agnostic gateway between AI agents and our enterprise data lake. Exposing a new dataset requires only a JSON metadata file that the server discovers automatically on the next pipeline sync.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we describe the architecture, show how a metadata-driven approach removes the need for per-dataset integration code, and explain how the serverless infrastructure supports multiple agents and applications in a multi-tenant environment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="problem-statement-and-design-goals"&gt;Problem statement and design goals&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two interrelated challenges shaped how we designed this system: fragmented data access patterns and an unsustainable onboarding model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="integration-challenge-at-scale"&gt;Integration challenge at scale&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our data lake contains thousands of datasets spanning operational metrics, business analytics, and domain-specific data. Multiple teams wanted AI assistants that could query this data conversationally, but each built its own integration from scratch. Each agent embedded its own SQL generation logic, schema knowledge, and connection handling, so a schema change meant patching every agent individually. Access control was fragmented, with no single audit point for data access decisions. Onboarding a new dataset required custom tool code, query validation, and redeployment, typically a multi-day effort per table.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="design-goals"&gt;Design goals&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We established four requirements:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configuration-driven data lake access. Adding a dataset requires only a metadata file, not code.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Agent-agnostic tool interfaces. Any MCP-compatible client can invoke the same tools.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Centralized permission enforcement. Access control is enforced at the MCP server layer.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Elastic infrastructure. The system scales with bursty AI workload patterns.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture separates concerns into four layers (client, MCP server, tools, and knowledge-base refresh), each independently scalable and deployable. A metadata catalog underpins the tools layer, storing schema descriptions, column-level semantics, and table relationships that the AI agent references when generating SQL. Because the agent derives schema knowledge from the catalog at query time, there is no need to hardcode table structures or redeploy when schemas change. The knowledge-base refresh layer keeps that catalog current, indexing new dataset metadata as it arrives. The following sections describe each layer in detail.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture-walkthrough"&gt;Architecture walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the request flow from user query to data layer across all four layers. A user submits a query from a client application (web chat, Slack, an IDE plugin, or an external agent). The request resolves through Amazon Route 53 for DNS and reaches an Application Load Balancer in a public subnet, configured with HTTPS and AWS WAF. The load balancer routes to Amazon Cognito, which authenticates the user and enriches the request. Authenticated traffic then enters the private subnet, where the client layer runs on Amazon ECS Fargate. This layer handles session state in Amazon DynamoDB and forwards requests through an internal Application Load Balancer to the MCP server layer, also on Amazon ECS Fargate. In the MCP server layer, an AI agent powered by Amazon Bedrock orchestrates tools built with FastMCP (a Python framework for building MCP servers). Amazon Bedrock Guardrails is applied to every model interaction. On the right, the data layer holds the tool backends. Amazon Athena and Amazon Redshift run SQL queries against the data lake, and Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, the fully managed Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) capability, handles semantic search. Data ingestion into Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) and the knowledge bases is handled by AWS Lambda. Each layer is described in detail in the sections that follow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-20343-1.jpeg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-20343-1.jpeg" alt="Architecture diagram of the serverless MCP server showing request flow from client applications through Route 53, AWS WAF, an Application Load Balancer, and Amazon Cognito into the client and MCP server layers on Amazon ECS Fargate, then to the data layer." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Request flow through the serverless MCP server, from client applications to the data layer&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="mcp-client-layer-authentication-and-session-management"&gt;MCP client layer: Authentication and session management&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A request flows from the client application through Amazon Route 53 for DNS resolution to an Application Load Balancer configured with HTTPS and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/waf/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS WAF&lt;/a&gt;. The load balancer forwards traffic to the Amazon ECS Fargate client service, which integrates with Amazon Cognito for JSON Web Token (JWT) authentication and Amazon DynamoDB for session storage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A context mapping configuration drives this enrichment, scoping each query to only the relevant tables. In our testing, this scoping keeps end-to-end interaction latency in the 15–20 second range. The following example shows a context mapping configuration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "package_data": {
    "description": "Package tracking and delivery information",
    "tables": ["analytics_db.regional_order_summary", "operations_gold.load_summary"],
    "sources": {"wiki": [], "quip": []}
  },
  "labor_planning": {
    "description": "Workforce management and capacity planning",
    "tables": ["workforce.capacity_details"],
    "sources": {"wiki": [], "quip": []}
  }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a context specifies tables, the MCP server narrows its search of the metadata catalog to those tables, reducing noise and improving SQL generation accuracy. Adding a new business domain requires only adding an entry to this mapping.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="mcp-server-layer-stateless-execution-with-conversation-state"&gt;MCP server layer: Stateless execution with conversation state&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The MCP server runs on its own ECS Fargate cluster, isolated from the client layer. Requests reach it through Amazon Route 53 and an internal Application Load Balancer in a private subnet. The server handles full orchestration including query analysis, tool selection, execution, and response generation. Through the MCP protocol endpoint (&lt;code&gt;/mcp&lt;/code&gt;), a FastMCP-powered JSON-RPC interface, external MCP-compatible clients such as Amazon Q Developer or IDE extensions can discover and invoke tools directly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Fargate tasks are stateless with conversation state persisted in DynamoDB, so tasks can be replaced or scaled without data loss. When a request arrives, the agent retrieves session history, analyzes user intent using Amazon Bedrock, discovers and invokes appropriate tools, and streams results back to the user.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tools-layer-and-metadata-driven-data-lake-access"&gt;Tools layer and metadata-driven data lake access&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each tool is registered with FastMCP and discoverable at runtime:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sr.no&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Backend&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Purpose&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SQL query tools&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;Amazon Redshift&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SQL queries against any configured dataset in the data lake and Redshift&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Document and metadata search&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Semantic search over internal documentation, table metadata and team wikis&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Policy search&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Search operational policies and standard procedures&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Athena query tools are the primary interface to the data lake. The agent first queries the metadata catalog to understand available schemas, generates an optimized SQL query through Amazon Bedrock, validates and auto-corrects the SQL for Athena compatibility, then executes it. Results flow back to the orchestrating agent for natural language synthesis.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="responsible-ai-controls"&gt;Responsible AI controls&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails is applied to all LLM interactions and is designed to filter harmful content, enforce grounding checks, and help block prompt injection attempts. These guardrails operate as production controls at the MCP server layer, so every connected agent benefits from the same content safety policies regardless of how it connects. The Athena query tools enforce &lt;code&gt;SELECT&lt;/code&gt;-only queries, rejecting write operations at the tool level before they reach the data lake.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="knowledge-base-refresh-layer-dataset-onboarding-with-a-single-json-file"&gt;Knowledge base refresh layer: Dataset onboarding with a single JSON file&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The entire system is built around configuration-driven extensibility. Adding a new dataset requires a metadata JSON file describing the table and an optional context mapping entry. The file captures the table’s schema, business context, and column-level semantics. It serves as the single source of truth that the AI agent uses to understand and query the dataset:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "database_service_name": "athena",
  "database_name": "analytics_db",
  "table_name": "regional_order_summary",
  "business_description_for_the_table": "Regional order history with shipping status and delivery performance. Contains completed orders with delivery data. Refreshes daily. In Athena SQL, always use date() to wrap string literals when comparing against timestamp columns.",
  "column_details": {
    "order_id [varchar]": {
      "description": "Unique order identifier assigned at checkout.",
      "sample_values": ["ORD-20250115-001234", "ORD-20250116-005678"]
    },
    "order_date [timestamp(3)]": {
      "description": "Date and time the order was placed. Use this column for time-range filtering.",
      "sample_values": ["2025-01-15 08:00:00.000", "2025-01-16 12:30:00.000"]
    },
    "warehouse_id [varchar]": {
      "description": "Fulfillment center that processed the order.",
      "sample_values": ["FC-EAST-07", "FC-WEST-03", "FC-SOUTH-12"]
    }
  },
  "load_type": "Incremental (refreshes last 7 days)",
  "refresh_frequency": "Daily at 6:00 AM UTC",
  "table_size": "~2.1B rows",
  "creation_time": "2024-03-15",
  "last_refresh_time": "2025-05-20T06:00:00Z"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;business_description_for_the_table&lt;/code&gt; field carries engine-specific query hints, such as “always use &lt;code&gt;date()&lt;/code&gt; to wrap string literals,” that reduce SQL validation failures. The &lt;code&gt;column_details&lt;/code&gt; field pairs each &lt;code&gt;column_name [type]&lt;/code&gt; with a natural language description and sample values, giving the agent both schema awareness and business semantics. The &lt;code&gt;sample_values&lt;/code&gt; field matters for accurate &lt;code&gt;WHERE&lt;/code&gt; clause generation because the agent learns real data patterns, for instance that &lt;code&gt;warehouse_id&lt;/code&gt; holds codes like &lt;code&gt;FC-EAST-07&lt;/code&gt; rather than spelled-out names like “East region facility.”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pipeline picks up metadata files, generates vector embeddings with Amazon Titan Text Embeddings, and indexes them into Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases. Once indexed, the table is discoverable by all connected agents without manual re-indexing or server restarts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To improve accuracy for specific applications, an engineer can optionally associate the dataset with a business context. This narrows the search scope, so the agent prioritizes the right tables. The following example shows a context mapping entry:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "delivery_metrics": {
    "description": "Delivery accuracy and defect attribution data",
    "tables": ["analytics_db.customer_orders_summary"],
    "sources": {"wiki": [], "quip": []}
  }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If no context is specified, the agent searches across all indexed tables. The context mapping reduces latency and improves SQL accuracy by limiting the search space. This scoping is optional and can be added at any time without modifying the metadata files themselves.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="putting-it-together-a-query-in-action"&gt;Putting it together: A query in action&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a user asks, “What were our top 10 highest-value orders last month?”, the client enriches the request with business context to narrow scope. The agent converts the question into a vector embedding, searches the metadata catalog for matching schemas, and selects the most relevant table via a lightweight LLM call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Using the selected schema, column descriptions, and engine-specific query hints, the agent generates a SQL statement such as:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT order_id, customer_id, total_amount, order_date
FROM analytics_db.regional_order_summary
WHERE order_date &amp;gt;= date_add('month', -1, current_date)
ORDER BY total_amount DESC
LIMIT 10&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The SQL is validated and auto-corrected for engine compatibility, then executed against Amazon Athena. The agent formats the results into a natural language response and streams it back to the user. In our testing, this end-to-end flow completes within 15–20 seconds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same orchestration extends beyond pure SQL. Because document search is registered as another tool on the same server, the agent can answer questions that span structured and unstructured sources in a single turn, such as “What was our return rate for Q4, and what does the product team’s analysis say about the root causes?” For that request, the agent runs an Athena query for the return-rate figure and a Knowledge Bases document search for the analysis, then combines both into one response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-and-multi-tenant-access"&gt;Security and multi-tenant access&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;That single-turn orchestration works only because every tool call passes through the same controlled gateway. The architecture uses layered security rather than a single enforcement point. The MCP server runs in private subnets behind an internal Application Load Balancer and is not reachable from the internet. Only the client layer, which sits behind a public-facing Application Load Balancer with AWS WAF, can route requests to it. Amazon Cognito validates user identity at the client layer, and only authenticated, token-bearing requests are forwarded downstream. At the tool level, the Athena query tools enforce &lt;code&gt;SELECT&lt;/code&gt;-only queries, rejecting write operations before they reach the data lake. All tool invocations are logged to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; with structured JSON, which gives security teams a single audit point for data access across every agent and application. This architecture is available in the AWS Regions where Amazon Bedrock and Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases are available.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This centralized design is also what makes the server multi-tenant. Because the standardized &lt;code&gt;/mcp&lt;/code&gt; endpoint accepts any MCP-compatible client regardless of which LLM or orchestration framework it uses, multiple agents share the same tools and the same access controls. Adding a new agent requires only pointing it at the server endpoint, so onboarding another team extends the system without weakening or duplicating the security model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="serverless-infrastructure-and-scaling"&gt;Serverless infrastructure and scaling&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each layer scales through a mechanism suited to its workload pattern:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Layer&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scaling mechanism&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trigger&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Client (ECS Fargate)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ECS Service Auto Scaling&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CPU utilization (50% threshold)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;MCP Server (ECS Fargate)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ECS Service Auto Scaling&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CPU/Memory utilization&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Athena&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Serverless (automatic)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Query concurrency&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Managed service scaling&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Inference requests&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon ECS Fargate provides serverless compute with no EC2 instances to manage. Downstream services (Athena, Bedrock) scale automatically, so the system handles a single query or hundreds of concurrent queries without configuration changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="use-cases-and-extensions"&gt;Use cases and extensions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This architecture supports several patterns beyond basic question-answering. Enterprise knowledge assistants can combine data lake queries with document search to answer complex questions spanning both structured data and unstructured knowledge, such as “What’s our return rate for Q4, and what does the product team’s analysis say about the root causes?” Internal analytics copilots let business users query the data lake conversationally without writing SQL, relying on the metadata descriptions and query hints to guide the agent toward accurate, performant query patterns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The standardized &lt;code&gt;/mcp&lt;/code&gt; endpoint also supports multi-agent workflows. Multiple specialized agents for data analysis, document retrieval, and operational alerting can share the same MCP server, each accessing the full tool suite through a common protocol. Onboarding a new team’s data follows the same configuration-driven pattern. They add a metadata JSON file, and within one pipeline sync cycle their dataset is discoverable by every connected agent and application.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A centralized MCP server on Amazon ECS Fargate provides a scalable, secure gateway between AI agents and enterprise data lakes. The metadata-driven approach removes the need for per-dataset integration code, and the Model Context Protocol standard means any MCP-compatible client can connect without custom adapters.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The key architectural decision is separating the tool layer from the agent layer. Agents evolve rapidly, but data access patterns should outlive any single agent implementation. Treating extensibility as a configuration problem rather than a code problem accelerates onboarding.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon ECS on AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/knowledge-base.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt; user guide, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/a&gt;, and the &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/2026-07-28/getting-started/intro" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol specification&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="next-steps"&gt;Next steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We are extending this architecture in two directions. First, finer-grained access control where table owners can mark sensitive columns as restricted per permission group, and the agent automatically excludes those columns from generated SQL without prompt-level changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Second, closing the feedback loop so that when queries fail or produce low-confidence results, the system captures the failure pattern and surfaces targeted suggestions for improving column descriptions, adding query hints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;
