		<title>Govern AI agent access with Okta Cross App Access and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore – Part 2</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/govern-ai-agent-access-with-okta-cross-app-access-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-2/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Hammad Ausaf]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 06 Nov 2026 17:09:23 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">568f99493ce9fc7f0fbe42c57947610f9ca4666c</guid>

					<description>In Part 1 you built one governed agent. Part 2 scales the pattern to an enterprise: Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway becomes the single front door and redeems Okta's grant, Cedar policies bind each tool to the agent, a second agent runs on Amazon EKS, and AgentCore Observability traces every allow and deny beside Okta's log in Amazon CloudWatch.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;In &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/govern-ai-agent-access-with-okta-cross-app-access-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-1/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1&lt;/a&gt;, you built a task agent on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. The agent runs on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and acts for signed-in users through Okta Cross App Access (XAA). Okta decided which agent might act for which user, and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, redeemed Okta’s grant inside the agent. That proves the governance mechanism, and it runs on a deliberately small topology: one agent, two Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers, and the authorization check written into each server.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The mechanism scales. The topology doesn’t. Three things that are fine for one agent and two tools become the work in an enterprise with hundreds of agents and thousands of tools:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What Part 1 does&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Why it stops scaling&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What Part 2 does instead&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The agent holds each MCP server’s runtime ARN and calls it directly&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;N agents against M servers is a wiring matrix, and every new tool touches every agent that needs it&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agents reach one Gateway endpoint, and the Gateway holds the target registry&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The agent’s role holds the token-exchange permission and reads the provider’s secret&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Every agent that acts for users gets a credential path to every resource it might reach&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The Gateway redeems the grant, and the agent’s role holds no token-exchange or secret permissions&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Each MCP server carries the same &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt; check in its own code&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The same rule, copied into thousands of servers, drifts and can’t be audited or changed in one place&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One default-deny Cedar policy set, evaluated at the Gateway before tools run&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you make that change on the Part 1 solution. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, becomes the single front door and redeems Okta’s grant for each target. Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore evaluates Cedar policies that bind each tool to the agent and the scope Okta delegated. A second agent on Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS) joins under its own Okta identity, to show that the front door doesn’t care where an agent runs. And Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Observability, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, with Amazon CloudWatch gives you one place to read every decision, including Okta’s. We also compare XAA with per-user OAuth consent for applications that can’t accept Okta’s grant.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-changes-from-part-1"&gt;What changes from Part 1&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Okta configuration, the AgentCore Identity credential provider, and the MCP servers from Part 1 stay as they are. Figure 1 shows the architecture with the additions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-1.png" alt="Part 1 solution extended with AgentCore Gateway, Policy, and Observability, a second agent on Amazon EKS, and the audit path" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The Part 1 solution with AgentCore Gateway, Policy, and Observability, a second agent on Amazon EKS, and the audit path, with numbers matching the steps that follow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The user signs in, and the backend for frontend (BFF) exchanges the ID token for an Identity Assertion JWT Authorization Grant (ID-JAG) issued to the task agent, agent A, as in Part 1.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The BFF invokes agent A on AgentCore runtime with the ID-JAG.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Agent A forwards the ID-JAG to its Gateway as the bearer token of its MCP session. The Gateway’s inbound authorizer accepts only agent A’s ID-JAGs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Policy in AgentCore evaluates Cedar policies for each tool listing and each tool call, before the Gateway invokes a target.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For calls that Cedar permits, the Gateway has AgentCore Identity redeem the ID-JAG for each target at Okta’s custom authorization server through the Part 1 credential provider. A call that Cedar denies doesn’t redeem the ID-JAG.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The MCP server checks the user’s groups and scope, as in Part 1, and records each decision as an &lt;code&gt;authz.check&lt;/code&gt; span.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Every hop emits OpenTelemetry spans to AgentCore Observability, which CloudWatch Transaction Search collects, and Okta streams its System Log to CloudWatch through Amazon EventBridge.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The reporting agent, agent B, runs on Amazon EKS and follows the same path through its own Gateway.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In addition to the Part 1 solution, you need the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prerequisite&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Needed for&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;eksctl, kubectl, and Docker&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The second agent on Amazon EKS&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Okta Log Streaming&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Okta’s System Log in CloudWatch&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AgentCore Gateways and policy engines, Amazon EKS clusters, CloudWatch log groups, and Amazon EventBridge rules&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The additions take about 90 minutes. The EKS control plane costs $0.10 per hour, plus nodes and the Amazon EKS Auto Mode management fee, and the AgentCore features charge for what you use. See &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eks/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EKS pricing&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore pricing&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As in Part 1, the base for this solution is the AWS sample &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize/08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain&lt;/a&gt; on GitHub, and you can build the Part 2 additions on top of it. Beyond that sample, the snippets and configurations in this post show the pattern conceptually. They aren’t a complete, deployable solution. The code is sample code for non-production use, so work with your security and legal teams to meet your organization’s security, regulatory, and compliance requirements before you deploy it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="create-a-gateway-that-accepts-the-id-jag"&gt;Create a Gateway that accepts the ID-JAG&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Gateway’s inbound authorizer works like the runtime authorizer from Part 1. Its issuer is Okta’s org authorization server, its audience is the custom authorization server, and &lt;code&gt;allowedClients&lt;/code&gt; pins it to one agent:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;authorizer = {"customJWTAuthorizer": {
    "discoveryUrl": f"{OKTA_ORG}/.well-known/openid-configuration",  # the ID-JAG's issuer
    "allowedAudience": [f"{OKTA_ORG}/oauth2/{CUSTOM_AS_ID}"],         # the resource's authorization server
    "allowedClients": [AGENT_CLIENT_ID],                               # only this agent's ID-JAGs
    "allowedScopes": ["xaa:read", "xaa:write"],
}}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 2 shows the resulting inbound authorizer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-2.png" alt="Gateway inbound authorizer accepting only ID-JAGs that Okta issued to the agent for the resource’s authorization server" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The Gateway accepts only ID-JAGs that Okta issued to the agent for the resource’s authorization server&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each MCP server becomes a Gateway target whose outbound authorization uses &lt;code&gt;TOKEN_EXCHANGE&lt;/code&gt; through the Part 1 credential provider, so the Gateway performs the second exchange:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;"credentialProviderConfigurations": [{
    "credentialProviderType": "OAUTH",
    "credentialProvider": {"oauthCredentialProvider": {
        "providerArn": provider_arn,          # the JWT_AUTHORIZATION_GRANT provider from Part 1
        "scopes": ["xaa:read", "xaa:write"],  # must equal the ID-JAG's scopes
        "grantType": "TOKEN_EXCHANGE",
    }},
}]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 3 shows a target configured this way.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-3.png" alt="Gateway target for the task manager configured to redeem the ID-JAG through the Part 1 credential provider" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The task manager target redeems the ID-JAG through the Part 1 credential provider&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three details matter here. First, as in Part 1, the target’s scopes must equal the ID-JAG’s. Second, give each target a static tool schema (&lt;code&gt;listingMode&lt;/code&gt; set to &lt;code&gt;DEFAULT&lt;/code&gt; with an inline &lt;code&gt;mcpToolSchema&lt;/code&gt;), so the tool names your Cedar policies reference are fixed when you write them. In our tests, policies that name tools also couldn’t attach to dynamically listed targets. Third, the Gateway pages &lt;code&gt;tools/list&lt;/code&gt; by target, so your client must follow the pagination cursor, or tools from later targets disappear. AgentCore Identity keeps the token from the Gateway’s exchange, so one single-use ID-JAG serves every tool call in a session. In our tests, it also opened a second session within its 5-minute life.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because a credential provider authenticates as one Okta agent, give each agent its own Gateway and provider. Sample &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize/08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain&lt;/a&gt; takes a different route, in which a Gateway interceptor performs both exchanges itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="bind-tools-to-agents-with-cedar"&gt;Bind tools to agents with Cedar&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Attach a Policy in AgentCore engine to the Gateway in &lt;code&gt;ENFORCE&lt;/code&gt; mode, and write permit-only policies. Cedar exposes JWT claims as tags. A policy that reads a missing tag errors and is skipped. A skipped &lt;code&gt;permit&lt;/code&gt; grants nothing, so default deny holds, but a skipped &lt;code&gt;forbid&lt;/code&gt; blocks nothing. Guard each tag with &lt;code&gt;hasTag&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-cedar"&gt;permit(
  principal is AgentCore::OAuthUser,
  action in [AgentCore::Action::"taskmanager___create_task",
             AgentCore::Action::"taskmanager___update_task_status"],
  resource == AgentCore::Gateway::"&amp;lt;gateway-arn&amp;gt;"
) when {
  principal.hasTag("client_id") &amp;amp;&amp;amp; principal.getTag("client_id") == "&amp;lt;agent-client-id&amp;gt;" &amp;amp;&amp;amp;
  principal.hasTag("scope") &amp;amp;&amp;amp;
  (principal.getTag("scope") == "xaa:write" ||
   principal.getTag("scope") like "xaa:write *" ||
   principal.getTag("scope") like "* xaa:write" ||
   principal.getTag("scope") like "* xaa:write *")
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The scope tag holds the ID-JAG’s space-separated &lt;code&gt;scope&lt;/code&gt; claim, so the four conditions match &lt;code&gt;xaa:write&lt;/code&gt; only as a whole entry. A scope such as &lt;code&gt;xaa:write-admin&lt;/code&gt; doesn’t match. A matching policy, written the same way, permits the read tools with &lt;code&gt;xaa:read&lt;/code&gt;. Check that every policy is &lt;code&gt;ACTIVE&lt;/code&gt; before you attach the engine: an engine whose policies failed to create leaves the Gateway denying every call. Cedar binds tools to the agent and its delegated scope, and the MCP server still decides each user’s authority from the &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt; claim, as in Part 1.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="point-the-agent-at-the-gateway"&gt;Point the agent at the Gateway&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agent A now forwards its inbound ID-JAG to the Gateway instead of calling AgentCore Identity itself:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;id_jag = context.request_headers["Authorization"].removeprefix("Bearer ")  # allowlisted header
tools = make_client(GATEWAY_URL, id_jag)  # the Gateway redeems the ID-JAG for each target&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Remove the Part 1 AgentCore Identity statements from the agent’s execution role. That’s the only change to the role, which now holds no token-exchange or secret permissions, because the Gateway’s role holds the permission to exchange tokens. Keep &lt;code&gt;allowedClients&lt;/code&gt; on the runtime’s authorizer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="run-a-second-agent-on-amazon-eks"&gt;Run a second agent on Amazon EKS&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our second agent, a reporting agent, runs the same code on Amazon EKS with its own Okta registration. Its resource connection allows only &lt;code&gt;xaa:read&lt;/code&gt;, and it has its own Gateway and credential provider. EKS Pod Identity gives the pod an IAM role that can invoke models and write its own logs and traces, and nothing else. The AWS Distro for OpenTelemetry (ADOT) SDK exports its traces. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/blob/2747451473d1fadbba18f5aee0524e533e3c2b2e/03-integrations/agents-hosted-outside-runtime/agents-on-eks/deploy.sh#L137-L143" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;agents-on-eks sample&lt;/a&gt; shows the Pod Identity setup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Outside AgentCore runtime, no authorizer sits in front of the pod, so the agent validates the ID-JAG before a model call. It checks the signature, issuer, audience, token type &lt;code&gt;oauth-id-jag+jwt&lt;/code&gt;, expiry, and that &lt;code&gt;client_id&lt;/code&gt; is its own. If you kept the Part 1 agent check in the MCP servers, add agent B’s client ID to &lt;code&gt;REGISTERED_AGENTS&lt;/code&gt;. Two settings tripped us up. The EKS Auto Mode general-purpose node pool runs only amd64, so build multi-architecture images. And the AgentCore Python SDK, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, binds to &lt;code&gt;localhost&lt;/code&gt; unless you set &lt;code&gt;DOCKER_CONTAINER=true&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="trace-every-decision-with-agentcore-observability"&gt;Trace every decision with AgentCore Observability&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The spans in this section are &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Observability&lt;/a&gt;, the component in Figure 1 that sits between the agents and CloudWatch. Runtime, Gateway, Identity, and Policy emit OpenTelemetry spans for each hop without code changes, after you turn on Transaction Search and trace delivery. Your own services add theirs with the AWS Distro for OpenTelemetry (ADOT). CloudWatch Transaction Search collects them, the AgentCore console reads them as agent traces, and Amazon CloudWatch Omni reads the same spans as &lt;code&gt;traces.default&lt;/code&gt;, which is what lets one query put them beside Okta’s events.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One tool call therefore produces this chain of spans, each naming the control that made a decision:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Span&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Emitted by&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What it records&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AgentCore.Gateway.InboundAuth&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Gateway&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Whether the inbound ID-JAG was issued to this agent&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AgentCore.Policy.AuthorizeAction&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Policy&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The Cedar decision for this tool&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AgentCore.Identity.GetWorkloadAccessToken&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Identity&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Binding the agent’s workload identity to the user&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AgentCore.Identity.GetResourceOauth2Token&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Identity&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Redeeming the ID-JAG at Okta for a scoped access token&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AgentCore.Gateway.InvokeTool&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Gateway&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The tool call and its outcome&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;authz.check&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The MCP server (ADOT)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The user’s groups, the agent, the decision, and the reason&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Okta’s System Log is the one record AWS can’t emit, so you bring it in. Figure 4 shows both paths meeting in Omni.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-4.png" alt="Audit path where AgentCore spans and Okta’s System Log both reach CloudWatch and one Omni query joins them" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: The audit path, where spans and Okta’s System Log reach CloudWatch and one Omni query joins them&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To set it up:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Turn on CloudWatch Transaction Search, turn on trace delivery for each Gateway (see this &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/blob/2747451473d1fadbba18f5aee0524e533e3c2b2e/02-use-cases/02-workflow-automation-agents/event-driven-claims-agent/scripts/enable_observability.py#L196-L222" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample script&lt;/a&gt;), and add ADOT to the BFF and the MCP servers. The task manager writes each decision, the user, and the agent onto an &lt;code&gt;authz.check&lt;/code&gt; span.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In Okta, add a Log Stream for Amazon EventBridge. Okta delivers each event to a partner event bus in your account, and a rule writes it to a CloudWatch Logs log group. In our tests, events arrived about 1 second after Okta published them. Each ID-JAG grant event names the agent as the actor, the user as a target, and the granted scopes and resource connection.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set up an Omni space in the account and AWS Region, add the Okta log group to the CloudWatch Dataset integration, and save a view, &lt;code&gt;view.xaa_authz_decisions&lt;/code&gt;, over the &lt;code&gt;authz.check&lt;/code&gt; spans.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One query then joins each ID-JAG grant to the decisions it led to:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT o.okta_time, o.agent, o.user_email, o.granted, a.decision_time, a.decision, a.reason
FROM (SELECT `@timestamp` AS okta_time, detail['actor']['id'] AS agent_id,
             detail['actor']['displayName'] AS agent,
             array_element(regexp_match(`@message`, '"type"\s*:\s*"User"\s*,\s*"alternateId"\s*:\s*"([^"]+)"'), 1) AS user_email,
             detail['debugContext']['debugData']['grantedScopes'] AS granted
      FROM "logs.default"
      WHERE `@timestamp` BETWEEN NOW() - INTERVAL '1' HOUR AND NOW()
        AND detail['eventType'] = 'app.oauth2.token.grant.id_jag') o
JOIN (SELECT `@timestamp` AS decision_time, user_id, agent_client_id, decision, reason
      FROM "view.xaa_authz_decisions"
      WHERE `@timestamp` BETWEEN NOW() - INTERVAL '1' HOUR AND NOW()) a
  ON a.user_id = o.user_email AND a.agent_client_id = o.agent_id
 AND a.decision_time BETWEEN o.okta_time AND o.okta_time + INTERVAL '5' MINUTE&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Alice, the result shows Okta granting the task agent &lt;code&gt;xaa:read xaa:write&lt;/code&gt; for her, then the MCP server denying her &lt;code&gt;create_task&lt;/code&gt; 16.7 seconds later and allowing &lt;code&gt;list_tasks&lt;/code&gt; after that, as Figure 5 shows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-5.png" alt="Omni query result joining Alice’s ID-JAG grant in Okta’s System Log to the two downstream authorization decisions" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: The Omni query joins Alice’s ID-JAG grant in Okta’s System Log to the two decisions it led to&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each request is also one trace, from the BFF through the agent, the Gateway’s identity and policy checks, and the MCP server’s decision, as Figure 6 shows. Omni marks the trace as a fault because the agent’s &lt;code&gt;create_task&lt;/code&gt; call returned the denial.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-6.png" alt="Single trace for Alice’s request from the agent’s tool call through the Gateway’s identity and Cedar checks to the MCP server’s deny" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: One trace for Alice’s request, from the agent’s tool call through the Gateway’s identity and Cedar checks to the MCP server’s deny&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two instrumentation details matter. ADOT finds the Region and signs its exports through &lt;code&gt;botocore&lt;/code&gt;, so add &lt;code&gt;botocore&lt;/code&gt; to services that lack it. Until we did, our MCP servers exported no spans. And ADOT’s agent mode turns off instrumentation for HTTP client libraries such as &lt;code&gt;requests&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;urllib3&lt;/code&gt; by default, so set &lt;code&gt;OTEL_PYTHON_DISABLED_INSTRUMENTATIONS&lt;/code&gt; in your BFF to override that default with a list that leaves out the BFF’s HTTP client (&lt;code&gt;requests&lt;/code&gt;, in ours), or its trace context won’t reach the agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="validate-the-results"&gt;Validate the results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We ran each check through both Gateways with the users from Part 1. Each session listed the tools, then called &lt;code&gt;list_tasks&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;create_task&lt;/code&gt;, and an employee lookup:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Caller and path&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tools listed&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;create_task&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reads&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice, agent A, Gateway A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;6&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Denied by the MCP server: requires &lt;code&gt;xaa-writers&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allowed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Bob, agent A, Gateway A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;6&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Created&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allowed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Carol, agent A, Gateway A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;6&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Denied by the MCP server: no &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt; claim&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allowed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Bob, agent B, Gateway B&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;4: Cedar hid the write tools&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Refused by the Gateway: “No policy applies to the request (denied by default)”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allowed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Through the deployed agents, the results matched: agent A denied Alice and Carol and created Bob’s task, and agent B told Bob it had no tool to create tasks. The same user got different authority through different agents, and Okta decided it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These requests stopped at the front door:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Request&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Response&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent B’s ID-JAG at Gateway A, or agent A’s at Gateway B&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;HTTP 403, MCP error -32002 &lt;code&gt;insufficient_scope&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An Okta access token instead of an ID-JAG&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;HTTP 403, the same error&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No token&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;HTTP 401, “Missing Bearer token”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent A’s ID-JAG sent to agent B on EKS&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Refused in 98 milliseconds, before a model call: “issued to a different agent”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Gateway returns the same generic error for a wrong agent and for the wrong token type, so check your configuration against the token’s &lt;code&gt;client_id&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;aud&lt;/code&gt; when you see it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Gateway’s identity and policy work is small next to the tool itself. Medians and 90th percentiles from 24 hours of Gateway A spans in Omni:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Span&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;p50&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;p90&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Gateway.InboundAuth&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;34 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;69 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Identity.Authorize&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;25 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;60 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Identity.GetWorkloadAccessToken&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;48 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;94 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Identity.GetResourceOauth2Token&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;70 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;439 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Policy.AuthorizeAction&lt;/code&gt; (Cedar, per call)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;47 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;56 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Policy.PartiallyAuthorizeActions&lt;/code&gt; (Cedar, per tool listing)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;65 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;75 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Gateway.TargetInvocation&lt;/code&gt; (the MCP server on AgentCore runtime)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;5,565 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;6,273 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The identity and policy spans for one tool call sum to about 0.2 seconds at their medians, and the MCP server’s own &lt;code&gt;authz.check&lt;/code&gt; span took under 1 millisecond. Gateway B showed the same pattern, with &lt;code&gt;GetResourceOauth2Token&lt;/code&gt; at 408 milliseconds at the median.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cross-app-access-or-per-user-consent"&gt;Cross App Access or per-user consent&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;XAA applies where the target application’s authorization server accepts an ID-JAG. There, your Okta administrator decides which agent may act for which users, users see no consent prompt, and Okta evaluates policy each time a token is requested. Applications with their own authorization servers, such as GitHub, rely on each user’s own OAuth consent instead. Figure 7 compares the two, as we measured them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-p2-7.png" alt="Comparison of Cross App Access and per-user OAuth consent, including the revocation behavior measured" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Cross App Access compared with per-user OAuth consent, with the revocation behavior we measured&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We deployed the AWS sample &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/2747451473d1fadbba18f5aee0524e533e3c2b2e/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize/07-consent-portal-auth-code-flow-targets" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;07-consent-portal-auth-code-flow-targets&lt;/a&gt; beside our stack, with Okta signing users in to an AgentCore Identity consent portal and a GitHub target on its own Gateway. Alice connected a GitHub test account through the portal, and Bob never connected one. The post &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/manage-end-user-oauth-consent-for-ai-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Manage end-user OAuth consent for AI agents with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; describes this lane in full. Here’s what we observed:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Action&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Next GitHub tool call&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice consented in the portal&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Returned her GitHub identity&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Bob, who never consented&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;MCP error -32042 with an AgentCore authorization URL&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice suspended in Okta, earlier token reused&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Still worked: the Gateway checks the inbound token’s signature and expiry, and that token lived 1 hour&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice suspended, new sign-in&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Refused&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice unsuspended&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Worked with no new consent&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice’s grant disconnected through the consent portal’s API&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Not connected, then -32042 again&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The two lanes revoke differently. In XAA, Okta’s controls stop the next ID-JAG mint, and issued tokens expire within about 5 minutes. In the consent lane, Okta controls only sign-in: the stored grant outlived the suspension and stayed until we disconnected it. Choose per target application, not once for the enterprise.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="where-this-pattern-applies"&gt;Where this pattern applies&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In media and entertainment, the fleet arrives quickly. A studio or streaming or games organization has agents for production tracking, title and asset metadata, rights and localization, and viewer or player support, each sitting in front of a different system with its own authorization model and its own auditors. The question isn’t whether one agent can be governed. It’s whether the hundredth one is governed the same way, and whether an administrator can still answer which agents reach which systems for which users.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Nothing here is specific to that industry. The agent is an identity in your IdP, the target is an OAuth 2.0 authorization server, and the Gateway is where authorization is decided. Teams in financial services, healthcare, and the public sector put different resources behind the same front door and get the same audit trail.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-next-observability-and-evaluations"&gt;What’s next: observability and evaluations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post uses AgentCore Observability for one purpose: to prove that each allow and deny traces back to a control. There’s more in it than that, and it’s a body of work in its own right. The spans already carry the model and tool latency behind every governance decision, Transaction Search can alert on a spike in denials, and AgentCore evaluations can score an agent’s behavior against your own criteria, online for live traffic or on demand over a chosen set of spans. A later post takes that up: what to measure on a governed agent, which evaluators catch an agent that reports success after a refused tool call, and how to run both without turning your traces into your largest bill.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the Part 2 resources, then follow the cleanup steps in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/govern-ai-agent-access-with-okta-cross-app-access-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-1/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1&lt;/a&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the Amazon EKS cluster (&lt;code&gt;eksctl delete cluster&lt;/code&gt;) and its Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) repository.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the Gateways and their targets, then the Policy in AgentCore engines, and then agent B’s credential provider.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the CloudWatch Omni space and domain, the Dataset integration, the Amazon EventBridge rule and partner event bus, and the CloudWatch log groups.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If you deployed sample 07, follow its cleanup steps.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the IAM roles and policies you created.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In Okta, delete the log stream, and deactivate and delete agent B and its linked app.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you moved the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/govern-ai-agent-access-with-okta-cross-app-access-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-1/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1&lt;/a&gt; solution from a topology that suits a few agents and tools to one that holds up across many. The Gateway became the single front door and redeemed Okta’s grant, so the agents held no tool credentials. Cedar policies evaluated by Policy in AgentCore replaced the check copied into each server. A second agent on Amazon EKS joined under its own Okta identity. And AgentCore Observability, with Okta’s System Log beside it in CloudWatch Omni, traced each allow and deny back to the agent, the user, and the control that decided it. The governance mechanism didn’t change, which is the point: only the topology around it did.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, explore the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/2747451473d1fadbba18f5aee0524e533e3c2b2e/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore samples&lt;/a&gt; and Okta’s &lt;a href="https://developer.okta.com/docs/concepts/xaa/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cross App Access documentation&lt;/a&gt;. For plain token exchange through a Gateway, read &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/implement-on-behalf-of-token-exchange-for-multi-tenant-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Implement on-behalf-of token exchange for multi-tenant agents with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
