			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Govern AI agent access with Okta Cross App Access and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore – Part 1</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/govern-ai-agent-access-with-okta-cross-app-access-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-1/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Hammad Ausaf]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 06 Nov 2026 17:09:18 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">c0f0c6e1e028e85f24cb91645e1a321ffca6e02a</guid>

					<description>An AI agent that acts for a user raises an identity question that per-app OAuth and service accounts leave open: which agent may act for which user, and how you stop it. In this first post of two, you build a task agent on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore that governs access through Okta Cross App Access and AgentCore Identity, and enforces each user's authority.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;An AI agent that updates a ticket or reads a customer record acts for a person, inside applications that person can reach. Your identity team then has to answer a question that per-app OAuth consent and shared service accounts leave open: which agent might act for which user in which application, and how you stop it. Okta Cross App Access (XAA) answers it in your identity provider (IdP). You register the agent in Okta, an administrator approves the applications it might reach, and Okta evaluates policy each time the agent asks to act for a user. In our tests, after an administrator removed a user’s assignment to the agent, Okta refused the agent’s new requests for that user within about 1 second, and tokens already issued expired within 5 minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; is a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. This solution uses two of its capabilities to carry out Okta’s decision on AWS. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, validates Okta’s grant. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, redeems it for a short-lived access token with credentials your agent code doesn’t see, and keeps that token in its token vault. In our tests, AgentCore Identity added about 0.6 seconds to a request that took about 30 seconds end to end.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, the first of two, you build a task agent on AgentCore runtime that acts for signed-in users through XAA and AgentCore Identity. You configure the AgentCore Identity parts it needs, enforce each user’s authority in the tools, and see what we measured: what each check refuses, the practices we settled on, and how fast Okta’s controls take effect. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/govern-ai-agent-access-with-okta-cross-app-access-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 2&lt;/a&gt; then puts the same governance behind Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, with Cedar policies, a second agent on Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS), and an audit trail in Amazon CloudWatch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Okta is the governance plane: it decides which agent might act for which user, against which resource. AgentCore Identity is the execution plane: it holds the agent’s credentials, turns Okta’s decision into a token the resource accepts, and keeps both identities in that token. In Figure 1, the agent and the two Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers it calls all run on AgentCore runtime.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-1.png" alt="Okta governance plane and AgentCore Identity execution plane, with the agent and two MCP servers running on AgentCore runtime" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Solution architecture, with numbers matching the steps that follow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each request carries three tokens, and each records a different decision: the ID token says who the user is, the Identity Assertion JWT Authorization Grant (ID-JAG) is Okta’s decision, and the access token is what the resource accepts. One request flows like this:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The user signs in to the web front end, a backend for frontend (BFF), with OpenID Connect through the agent’s linked Okta app.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For each request, the BFF exchanges the user’s ID token for an ID-JAG at Okta’s org authorization server, authenticated as the agent (RFC 8693 token exchange). Okta checks that the agent is active, that the user is assigned to it, and that an administrator connected it to the target authorization server for the requested scopes. The ID-JAG is single-use and expires in 5 minutes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The BFF invokes the agent on AgentCore runtime with the ID-JAG as its bearer token. The runtime’s JWT authorizer accepts only ID-JAGs that Okta issued to this agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The runtime gets a workload access token from AgentCore Identity that binds the agent’s workload identity to the user, and passes it to the agent code. This workload access token is an AWS-internal binding token: neither Okta nor the resource sees it, so it isn’t one of the three tokens.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The agent asks AgentCore Identity for an access token. AgentCore Identity redeems the ID-JAG at the resource’s Okta custom authorization server with the JWT authorization grant (RFC 7523), authenticated as the agent. Okta returns a 5-minute access token that names both the user and the agent and carries the user’s groups.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The agent calls the MCP servers with that token. The task manager checks that a registered agent obtained it, and that the user’s group and scope allow the tool.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 2 shows the same request as a sequence, with the check that each party makes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-2.png" alt="Sequence of the three-token chain showing the ID token, ID-JAG, and access token, and the check each party makes" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The three-token chain and the check each party makes, with the step 4 workload access token staying inside AWS&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow along, you need the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prerequisite&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What we used&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An AWS account with access to Amazon Bedrock AgentCore and an Anthropic Claude model in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;US West (Oregon)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An Okta org with Cross App Access for AI agents and API Access Management (custom authorization servers)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An Okta Integrator Free Plan org&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Python, the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI), boto3, the AgentCore CLI, and the AgentCore Python SDK, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Python 3.11, AWS CLI version 2, boto3 1.43, AgentCore CLI 0.31.1, and AgentCore Python SDK 1.24.1&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Permission to create AgentCore resources and IAM roles&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The walkthrough takes about 1 hour. AgentCore charges for what you use. See &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore pricing&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The base for this solution is the AWS sample &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize/08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain&lt;/a&gt; in the AgentCore samples repository on GitHub, and you can build the solution in this post on top of it. Beyond that sample, the snippets and configurations in this post show the pattern conceptually. They aren’t a complete, deployable solution. The code is sample code for non-production use, so work with your security and legal teams to meet your organization’s security, regulatory, and compliance requirements before you deploy it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="register-the-agent-in-okta"&gt;Register the agent in Okta&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Okta needs an authorization server for the resource, the agent, a connection between them, and a policy that admits the agent’s grant. Do all of these steps in the Okta Admin Console: registering the agent creates its linked app, so you don’t need an app integration wizard.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create a custom authorization server for the resource application with the audience &lt;code&gt;api://task-manager&lt;/code&gt; and the custom scopes &lt;code&gt;xaa:read&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;xaa:write&lt;/code&gt;. Request only the resource’s custom scopes: Okta rejects an ID-JAG request that includes OpenID Connect scopes such as &lt;code&gt;openid&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;profile&lt;/code&gt; (&lt;code&gt;invalid_scope&lt;/code&gt;), so a request needs at least one custom scope and no OpenID Connect scopes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Add a &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt; claim to its access tokens, filtered to groups that start with &lt;code&gt;xaa-&lt;/code&gt;. This claim carries each user’s authority to the tools.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the Admin Console, go to &lt;strong&gt;Directory&lt;/strong&gt; &amp;gt; &lt;strong&gt;AI Agents&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Register AI agent&lt;/strong&gt; and then &lt;strong&gt;Register manually&lt;/strong&gt;, optionally assign an owner, and create a linked OpenID Connect app. On &lt;strong&gt;Client registration&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Client secret&lt;/strong&gt;, generate a secret, and then activate the agent. The agent and the app share one client ID, and that app must sign your users in: an ID token from another app fails the exchange with “the client application is not registered for delegation to this agent.”&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the agent’s &lt;strong&gt;Resource connections&lt;/strong&gt; tab, connect it to the authorization server, limited to the scopes it needs, as Figure 3 shows.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Add an access policy rule to the custom authorization server that allows the agent’s JWT Bearer grant, with a 5-minute access token lifetime. Without the grant type, the second exchange fails with &lt;code&gt;no_matching_policy&lt;/code&gt;, and the lifetime bounds how long a revoked delegation keeps working.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Assign the users or groups who might act through the agent. They sign in through the linked app, so give it an app sign-in policy that admits them with the authenticators they’ve enrolled. In our sandbox org, the linked app had its own policy with a rule for the test group. For real users, apply your organization’s policy, including multifactor authentication.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-3.png" alt="Okta resource connection for the task agent, limited to the xaa:read and xaa:write scopes" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The task agent’s resource connection in Okta, limited to the xaa:read and xaa:write scopes&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Okta’s &lt;a href="https://developer.okta.com/docs/guides/xaa-agent-to-app/main/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cross App Access guide&lt;/a&gt; covers the console steps, and the AWS sample &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/2747451473d1fadbba18f5aee0524e533e3c2b2e/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize/08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain&lt;/a&gt; scripts the authorization server and its JWT bearer policy. The BFF then requests the ID-JAG, authenticated as the agent:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-http"&gt;POST /oauth2/v1/token HTTP/1.1
Host: YOUR-ORG.okta.com
Authorization: Basic &amp;lt;the agent's client ID and secret&amp;gt;
Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded

grant_type=urn:ietf:params:oauth:grant-type:token-exchange
&amp;amp;requested_token_type=urn:ietf:params:oauth:token-type:id-jag
&amp;amp;subject_token=&amp;lt;the user's ID token&amp;gt;
&amp;amp;subject_token_type=urn:ietf:params:oauth:token-type:id_token
&amp;amp;audience=https://YOUR-ORG.okta.com/oauth2/YOUR-CUSTOM-AS-ID
&amp;amp;scope=xaa:read xaa:write&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The AWS sample &lt;code&gt;06-okta-xaa&lt;/code&gt; sends the same request in Python (&lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/blob/2747451473d1fadbba18f5aee0524e533e3c2b2e/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize/06-okta-xaa/agent/xaa_client.py#L125-L140" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;agent/xaa_client.py&lt;/a&gt;). The BFF authenticates as the agent, so store the agent’s client secret in AWS Secrets Manager rather than in code or configuration files.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="set-up-agentcore-identity"&gt;Set up AgentCore Identity&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Identity holds three things for this solution, and your agent code doesn’t see the client secret or call Okta’s token endpoint:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Part of AgentCore Identity&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What it holds&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Who creates it&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Workload identity&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The agent’s identity on AWS, bound to the signed-in user for each request&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AgentCore runtime, for each agent runtime&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;OAuth2 credential provider&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The agent’s Okta client ID and secret, and the grant to use&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;You, once per Okta agent&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Token vault&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The access tokens the provider obtains&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AgentCore Identity&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="create-the-credential-provider"&gt;Create the credential provider&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The credential provider performs the second exchange. Point it at the custom authorization server, authenticate it as the AI agent, and set the on-behalf-of grant to &lt;code&gt;JWT_AUTHORIZATION_GRANT&lt;/code&gt;, which AgentCore Identity sends as &lt;code&gt;jwt-bearer&lt;/code&gt; with the ID-JAG as the assertion:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;control = boto3.client("bedrock-agentcore-control")
provider = control.create_oauth2_credential_provider(
    name="okta-xaa-obo",
    credentialProviderVendor="CustomOauth2",
    oauth2ProviderConfigInput={"customOauth2ProviderConfig": {
        "oauthDiscovery": {"discoveryUrl": f"{OKTA_ORG}/oauth2/{CUSTOM_AS_ID}/.well-known/openid-configuration"},
        "clientId": AGENT_CLIENT_ID,          # the AI agent's Okta client ID
        "clientSecret": AGENT_CLIENT_SECRET,  # AgentCore Identity stores it in AWS Secrets Manager
        "clientAuthenticationMethod": "CLIENT_SECRET_BASIC",
        "onBehalfOfTokenExchangeConfig": {"grantType": "JWT_AUTHORIZATION_GRANT"},
    }},
)
provider_arn = provider["credentialProviderArn"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Okta binds both exchanges to the client named in the ID-JAG, so each Okta agent needs its own provider: when we redeemed one agent’s ID-JAG as another, Okta refused with “The ‘client_id’ in the JWT Bearer Grant must match the ‘client_id’ used to authenticate the client.” AgentCore Identity keeps the secret in a service-managed AWS Secrets Manager secret, which you rotate with &lt;code&gt;update-oauth2-credential-provider&lt;/code&gt;. If your team already manages the client secret in Secrets Manager, you can reference that secret’s ARN instead. AgentCore Identity reads the client secret from it, so you can rotate the secret without updating the provider (see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reference-your-own-aws-secrets-manager-secrets-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-identity/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Reference your own AWS Secrets Manager secrets in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt;). To avoid a shared secret, authenticate with a private key instead. See &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/authenticate-with-private-key-jwt-using-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-identity/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Authenticate with Private Key JWT using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt;. Figure 4 shows the provider in the AgentCore console.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/ML-21547-4.png" alt="AgentCore console view of the credential provider configured with the JWT authorization grant" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: The credential provider authenticating as the agent with the JWT authorization grant&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="use-the-workload-identity-that-agentcore-runtime-manages"&gt;Use the workload identity that AgentCore runtime manages&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore runtime creates and manages a workload identity for each agent runtime. When a request passes the runtime’s JWT authorizer, the runtime &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/get-workload-access-token.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;gets a workload access token&lt;/a&gt; for that workload identity and the inbound token’s issuer and subject, and passes it to your code. AgentCore Identity then uses the ID-JAG behind it as the assertion for the exchange.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The AgentCore Python SDK keeps the workload access token in &lt;code&gt;BedrockAgentCoreContext&lt;/code&gt;, not in the request headers that your entrypoint receives. Our first version looked for it in the headers, found nothing, and fell back to its own &lt;code&gt;GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT&lt;/code&gt; call with a second workload identity, on all 21 requests we checked. Read it from the context instead:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from bedrock_agentcore.runtime import BedrockAgentCoreContext

identity = boto3.client("bedrock-agentcore")

def okta_access_token() -&amp;gt; str:
    workload_token = BedrockAgentCoreContext.get_workload_access_token()  # minted by the runtime
    return identity.get_resource_oauth2_token(
        workloadIdentityToken=workload_token,
        resourceCredentialProviderName="okta-xaa-obo",
        scopes=["xaa:read", "xaa:write"],  # must equal the ID-JAG's scopes
        oauth2Flow="ON_BEHALF_OF_TOKEN_EXCHANGE",
    )["accessToken"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Identity keeps the access token in its token vault for the rest of the request. Across 10 requests, the agent’s first call redeemed the ID-JAG in about half a second (see the latency table in Validate the results), and the second, for the other MCP server, returned the same token in a median of 58 milliseconds without redeeming the single-use ID-JAG again. A new ID-JAG for the same user got a new token, so each request reflects Okta’s latest decision. The sample &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/2747451473d1fadbba18f5aee0524e533e3c2b2e/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize/08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain&lt;/a&gt; reads the workload access token the same way (&lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/blob/2747451473d1fadbba18f5aee0524e533e3c2b2e/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize/08-okta-xaa-delegation-chain/agent/agent.py#L86-L101" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;agent/agent.py&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="scope-the-agents-role-to-its-workload-identity-and-provider"&gt;Scope the agent’s role to its workload identity and provider&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Identity doesn’t bind workload identities to credential providers in the same account. The agent’s IAM role does, so &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/scope-credential-provider-access.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;scope it&lt;/a&gt; to the agent’s own workload identity and provider, and to that provider’s secret:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;[
  {
    "Sid": "OktaOnBehalfOf",
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": "bedrock-agentcore:GetResourceOauth2Token",
    "Resource": [
      "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-west-2:123456789012:workload-identity-directory/default",
      "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-west-2:123456789012:workload-identity-directory/default/workload-identity/RUNTIME-WORKLOAD-IDENTITY",
      "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-west-2:123456789012:token-vault/default",
      "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-west-2:123456789012:token-vault/default/oauth2credentialprovider/okta-xaa-obo"
    ]
  },
  {
    "Sid": "ReadProviderSecret",
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": "secretsmanager:GetSecretValue",
    "Resource": "arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-west-2:123456789012:secret:bedrock-agentcore-identity!default/oauth2/okta-xaa-obo-*"
  }
]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Take the workload identity’s name from &lt;code&gt;workloadIdentityDetails&lt;/code&gt; in &lt;code&gt;GetAgentRuntime&lt;/code&gt;, and the provider’s ARN from &lt;code&gt;credentialProviderArn&lt;/code&gt;: the segment is &lt;code&gt;oauth2credentialprovider&lt;/code&gt;, and with the hyphenated form every call failed with &lt;code&gt;AccessDeniedException&lt;/code&gt;. The role needs no &lt;code&gt;GetWorkloadAccessToken&lt;/code&gt; permission, because the runtime gets that token, not the agent. Our tests passed with only these two statements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deploy-the-agent-on-agentcore-runtime"&gt;Deploy the agent on AgentCore runtime&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Configure the agent runtime’s inbound authorizer to accept only ID-JAGs that Okta issued to this agent. Its issuer is Okta’s org authorization server, and its audience is the resource’s custom authorization server. In &lt;code&gt;agentcore.json&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;"authorizerType": "CUSTOM_JWT",
"authorizerConfiguration": {
  "customJwtAuthorizer": {
    "discoveryUrl": "https://YOUR-ORG.okta.com/.well-known/openid-configuration",
    "allowedAudience": ["https://YOUR-ORG.okta.com/oauth2/YOUR-CUSTOM-AS-ID"],
    "allowedClients": ["YOUR_AGENT_CLIENT_ID"],
    "allowedScopes": ["xaa:read", "xaa:write"]
  }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;allowedClients&lt;/code&gt; matches the &lt;code&gt;client_id&lt;/code&gt; claim that an ID-JAG carries. Without it, the runtime admitted another agent’s ID-JAG with HTTP 200 and the request failed later, at the exchange. With it, the runtime refused that token with HTTP 401.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Besides the policy from the previous section, the execution role needs &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModel&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt;. It needs no &lt;code&gt;bedrock-agentcore:InvokeAgentRuntime&lt;/code&gt; permission for the MCP servers: each runs on AgentCore runtime behind its own JWT authorizer, with the custom authorization server as issuer and &lt;code&gt;api://task-manager&lt;/code&gt; as audience, so the agent calls it with the Okta access token as a bearer token, not with AWS Signature Version 4 (SigV4).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="enforce-each-users-authority-in-the-mcp-server"&gt;Enforce each user’s authority in the MCP server&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The resource connection sets the scopes for every user of an agent, so all three test users received &lt;code&gt;xaa:read xaa:write&lt;/code&gt;, and per-user authority travels in the &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt; claim instead: Alice’s token carried &lt;code&gt;xaa-readers&lt;/code&gt;, Bob’s &lt;code&gt;xaa-writers&lt;/code&gt;, and Carol’s had none.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The task manager checks two things: it accepts only tokens a registered agent obtained, and it allows a write only when the group and the scope are both present:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;REGISTERED_AGENTS = {"YOUR_AGENT_CLIENT_ID"}

def _require_agent() -&amp;gt; None:
    claims = _decode_claims()  # the runtime's JWT authorizer already validated the token
    agent = (claims.get("act") or {}).get("sub")
    if agent in REGISTERED_AGENTS and claims.get("cid") == agent:
        return
    raise ToolError("insufficient_privileges: this server accepts only tokens that a registered "
                    "AI agent obtained through Okta Cross App Access")

def _require_write() -&amp;gt; None:
    _require_agent()
    if WRITERS_GROUP in _groups() and "xaa:write" in _scopes():
        return
    raise ToolError(f"insufficient_privileges: this tool requires membership in '{WRITERS_GROUP}'")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both checks close gaps we found: a write check that allowed tokens without a &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt; claim let Carol, who belongs to neither group, create tasks, and before &lt;code&gt;_require_agent&lt;/code&gt; a plain token-exchange token, issued to a service app with no &lt;code&gt;act&lt;/code&gt; claim, created a task for Bob. The server can read the claims without verifying the token again only because the runtime’s JWT authorizer already verified it. A server without that authorizer in front of it must check the signature, issuer, audience, and expiry itself, as the Part 2 agent on Amazon EKS does.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="validate-the-results"&gt;Validate the results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We ran every check against the deployed solution. Alice, Bob, and Carol each asked the agent to create a task and then list the project’s tasks:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Caller&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;create_task&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;list_tasks&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Denied: requires membership in &lt;code&gt;xaa-writers&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allowed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Bob&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Created&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allowed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Carol&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Denied: no &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt; claim&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allowed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Bob, then Alice right after him&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Created for Bob, then denied for Alice: no authority carried over&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allowed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These requests stopped before tools ran:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Request&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Stopped by&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Response&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent B’s ID-JAG sent to the task agent&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The runtime’s JWT authorizer&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;401: “Claim ‘client_id’ value mismatch with configuration.”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An ID-JAG held for 245 seconds&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The runtime’s JWT authorizer&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;401: “Ineffectual token, will expire within the next minute”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A malformed bearer token&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The runtime’s JWT authorizer&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;403: “Failed to parse token”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One ID-JAG redeemed twice&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Okta’s custom authorization server&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;400: “The id-jag was already used.”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A narrower scope at the second exchange&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Okta’s custom authorization server&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;400: “The scope parameter must exactly match the scopes in the id-jag.”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A scope outside the connection&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Okta’s org authorization server&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;400 &lt;code&gt;invalid_scope&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A plain token exchange token at the MCP server&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The MCP server&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Denied: only tokens that a registered AI agent obtained&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The token that AgentCore Identity returned matched one we redeemed directly at Okta, claim for claim: &lt;code&gt;sub&lt;/code&gt; was the user, &lt;code&gt;cid&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;act&lt;/code&gt; named the agent, &lt;code&gt;scp&lt;/code&gt; held the connection’s scopes, &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt; held the user’s groups, and it lived 300 seconds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Identity adds about 0.6 seconds to a request that takes about 30 seconds end to end, most of it model and tool calls. Medians of five runs from our test client:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Call&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Median&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Sign-in, scripted (authorization code flow)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1,205 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ID token to ID-JAG at Okta’s org authorization server&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;216 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;135 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;GetResourceOauth2Token&lt;/code&gt;, including Okta’s JWT bearer grant&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;508 ms&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="govern-agents-in-operation"&gt;Govern agents in operation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Okta evaluates the agent, the user, and the connection when it mints an ID-JAG. We measured what each administrator action does to the next request and to issued tokens:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Admin action in Okta&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;New requests&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tokens already issued&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Delete the agent’s resource connection&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Refused immediately (&lt;code&gt;invalid_target&lt;/code&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Work until they expire, up to 5 minutes&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Remove the user’s assignment&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Refused within about 1 second, even with an ID token the user already held&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Up to 5 minutes&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Deactivate the agent&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Refused after Okta runs the operation, which our test org queued for 86 minutes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Up to 5 minutes&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Actions on one agent left the other working. Okta doesn’t re-check the user when an ID-JAG is redeemed: its custom authorization server issued an access token 3 seconds after we removed a user, for an ID-JAG minted before the removal. Okta’s System Log records each grant with the agent as the actor, and Part 2 streams those events into CloudWatch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="best-practices-for-the-delegation-chain"&gt;Best practices for the delegation chain&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Six practices came out of these measurements, each one a property of the chain rather than a preference:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Practice&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What we measured&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Mint a fresh ID-JAG for each request&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An ID-JAG is single-use and lives 300 seconds. The runtime refuses one with under 60 seconds left, so 225 seconds worked, 245 didn’t&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Carry per-user authority in the &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt; claim, and request one scope set per request&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Okta sets scopes from the resource connection, so all three users got the same scopes, and the second exchange must request exactly the ID-JAG’s&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Pin the agent at the runtime, and check &lt;code&gt;act&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;cid&lt;/code&gt; in the tool&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;allowedClients&lt;/code&gt; matches the &lt;code&gt;client_id&lt;/code&gt; claim an ID-JAG carries. Okta access tokens carry &lt;code&gt;cid&lt;/code&gt; instead&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reproduce a failed exchange directly against Okta&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Through AgentCore Identity, a scope mismatch and a wrong client both surface as “Token exchange failed with HTTP status 400”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Grant the agent’s role read access to the provider’s secret&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;GetResourceOauth2Token&lt;/code&gt; failed until the role could read it&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Keep lifetimes short, and use the resource connection as your kill switch&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Deleting the connection took effect immediately. Deactivating the agent sat in Okta’s beta AI agent API queue for 86 minutes, with no cancel&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cross-app-access-compared-with-plain-token-exchange"&gt;Cross App Access compared with plain token exchange&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Plain RFC 8693 token exchange also carries the user’s identity downstream. We compared it with XAA against the same Okta authorization server and user:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Behavior we observed&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Plain token exchange&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cross App Access&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent identity in the token&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No &lt;code&gt;act&lt;/code&gt; claim&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;act&lt;/code&gt; names the agent&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Scopes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Widened: Alice’s own token had &lt;code&gt;xaa:read&lt;/code&gt;, and the exchange returned &lt;code&gt;xaa:read xaa:write&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The scopes the administrator approved&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Exchange rule limited to &lt;code&gt;xaa-writers&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice still received a token&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Alice denied&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;User unassigned, earlier token reused&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Still exchanged&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Refused&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;What administrators see&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A service app with a grant type&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The agent, its connections, and the agent in each log event&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These results reflect our Okta configuration. Okta’s guide shows an &lt;code&gt;act&lt;/code&gt; claim for exchanged tokens, which we didn’t receive. Plain token exchange carried the user’s identity, but not the user’s authority.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="where-this-pattern-applies"&gt;Where this pattern applies&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We work with media and entertainment companies, and this is where we met the problem first. A studio or streaming or games organization runs agents against production tracking, title and asset metadata, rights and localization, and viewer or player support. Each system has its own authorization model and its own auditors, and a user’s authority differs by title, territory, or release window, so “which agent might act for which user in which system” has to be answered before an agent ships.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Nothing in the chain is specific to that industry: the agent is an identity in your IdP, the target is an OAuth 2.0 authorization server, and the user’s authority rides in a claim. Financial services, healthcare, and public sector teams put different resources behind the same controls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the resources you created:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the AWS CloudFormation stacks that the AgentCore CLI created for the agent and the two MCP servers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the credential provider with &lt;code&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control delete-oauth2-credential-provider --name okta-xaa-obo&lt;/code&gt;, and any workload identity you created yourself with &lt;code&gt;delete-workload-identity&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the IAM policies you added to the execution roles.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In Okta, deactivate and delete the AI agent and its linked app, and remove the resource connection, the authorization server’s policy rule, scopes, and claim, and the test users and groups.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you used Okta Cross App Access to decide which agent might act for which user, and AgentCore Identity to carry out each decision. The runtime admitted only the agent’s own ID-JAGs, the credential provider redeemed each one with a secret the agent didn’t hold, the token vault reused the result within a request, and the MCP server enforced each user’s authority from the &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt; claim.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This design keeps the exchange in the agent’s role and repeats the authorization check in every MCP server. See &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/govern-ai-agent-access-with-okta-cross-app-access-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 2 of this series&lt;/a&gt; to learn why its design moves both behind one AgentCore Gateway with Cedar policies, adds a second agent on Amazon EKS, and joins the Okta System Log with AgentCore traces in CloudWatch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, explore the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/2747451473d1fadbba18f5aee0524e533e3c2b2e/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore samples&lt;/a&gt; and Okta’s &lt;a href="https://developer.okta.com/docs/concepts/xaa/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cross App Access documentation&lt;/a&gt;. For the API details, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/on-behalf-of-token-exchange.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;On-behalf-of token exchange&lt;/a&gt; in the AgentCore Developer Guide.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Greenlight Financial built an autonomous SOC solution on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-greenlight-financial-built-an-autonomous-soc-solution-on-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Adam Maksimuk]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 06 Nov 2026 16:55:05 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">c5e92a0371483b3162fb07723de361bea61a1cce</guid>

					<description>Greenlight Financial resolves more than 90% of up to 4,000 monthly security and risk cases without human intervention using Luna, a tiered multi-agent system on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. This post covers the architecture, episodic memory for continuous learning, and the expansion from SOC operations into financial risk triage.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Greenlight Financial resolves more than 90 percent of up to 4,000 security and risk cases each month without human intervention. Greenlight’s Luna uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, infrastructure for building, connecting, and optimizing agents at scale. Specifically, Greenlight uses AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, to host Luna’s agent code. It uses AgentCore memory, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, to support its tiered multi-agent system. This avoids the need to build and operate these capabilities in-house. Luna triages dark web breach-card alerts, identity anomalies, and financial-risk events in seconds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post explains how Greenlight Financial built an autonomous security operations center (SOC) solution on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. We cover its multi-tiered supervisor architecture, more than 30 specialized agents, episodic memory for continuous learning, and expansion from SOC use cases into financial risk triage. We share production metrics, infrastructure decisions, and patterns for building autonomous security operations in regulated industries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-greenlight-needed-to-solve"&gt;What Greenlight needed to solve&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Greenlight Financial provides financial products for families: debit cards, savings tools, and connected devices such as the Family Hub and Pet Tracker. The company processes sensitive financial data at scale under the same regulatory requirements as major banks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Greenlight’s SOC receives 3,000–4,000 security and risk alerts per month from four primary vectors:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dark web breach cards&lt;/strong&gt; – Card Network and internal threat intel flag Greenlight customer cards found on the dark web.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Identity and access anomalies&lt;/strong&gt; – Unusual login patterns, privilege escalations, and policy violations across Identity Provider (IdP) and internal systems.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Infrastructure alerts&lt;/strong&gt; – Signals from Signals from EDR (Endpoint Detection and Response), SIEM (Security Information and Event Management), Asset Inventory, and other observability tools.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Financial risk events&lt;/strong&gt; – Suspicious transaction patterns, compliance triggers, and fraud indicators from card processing systems.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each alert demands cross-referencing multiple data sources before an analyst can render a verdict. A single dark web breach card notification requires checking whether the card is active, deduplicating against prior notifications, reviewing customer session data, and checking open support tickets. This workflow takes a human analyst 15-30 minutes per case. At 4,000 cases per month, that math breaks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Greenlight needed a system that could:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Triage and resolve routine cases autonomously at machine speed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Escalate complex cases with full context to human analysts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Learn from resolution patterns to improve accuracy over time.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Operate within financial services compliance guardrails.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture-tiered-multi-agent-system-on-amazon-bedrock-agentcore"&gt;Architecture: Tiered multi-agent system on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Greenlight built Luna as a hierarchical multi-agent system on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. The architecture follows a tiered supervisor pattern that mirrors how human SOC teams operate. L1 handles volume, and L2 handles complexity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/c-ml-21973-arch.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/c-ml-21973-arch.png" alt="Luna’s tiered multi-agent architecture, with a Tier 1 supervisor orchestrating specialized agents and escalating complex cases to a Tier 2 supervisor" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Luna’s tiered supervisor architecture on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tier-1-supervisor-high-volume-triage"&gt;Tier 1 supervisor: High-volume triage&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tier 1 supervisor uses an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-supported.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;advanced language model available through Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; running on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime within an Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC). The runtime uses private subnets and direct code deployment through a ZIP file and Amazon Simple Storage Service (&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3&lt;/a&gt;). It handles initial triage of incoming cases by orchestrating more than 30 specialized agents:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Domain&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What the agent does&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Identity and Access&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Checks login history, MFA status, session anomalies&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Endpoint Detection&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Pulls endpoint alerts, process trees, IOC matches&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Observability&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Correlates infrastructure metrics with alert timing&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Asset Inventory&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Maps affected assets, ownership, exposure surface&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Data Analytics&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Queries historical patterns and baseline deviations&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Payment Processing&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Verifies card status, transaction history, account state&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Customer Experience&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Reviews customer session behavior around incident time&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Customer Support&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Checks related open tickets, prior complaints&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Code and Repositories&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Traces code changes correlated with alerts&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Project Tracking&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Links incidents to recent deployments or known issues&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Threat Intelligence&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Enriches IOCs with external threat context&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Automation (SOAR)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Executes approved remediation playbooks&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Tier 1 supervisor receives cases through two paths: Amazon API Gateway, which exposes the Luna Queue API and a Model Context Protocol (MCP) endpoint, and Tines SOAR for automated alert ingestion. It determines which specialized agents to invoke based on alert type, synthesizes their findings, and renders a verdict. The median triage time for routine cases is single-digit seconds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tier-2-supervisor-deep-dive-investigation"&gt;Tier 2 supervisor: Deep-dive investigation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cases that exceed Tier 1’s confidence threshold escalate to a Tier 2 supervisor using an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-supported.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;advanced foundation model on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; with extended reasoning capabilities. This agent handles the 10 percent of cases that require multi-step reasoning:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Re-investigates escalated cases with deeper cross-source correlation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Invokes specialized agents through &lt;code&gt;boto3&lt;/code&gt; with expanded context windows.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Chains reasoning across 3–5 data sources to establish causality.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Produces detailed investigation reports with evidence chains for human review.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Tier 2 supervisor runs on a scheduled basis, using Amazon EventBridge rate triggers and Amazon Simple Notification Service (Amazon SNS) queue completions to batch-process escalated cases, and on demand for urgent Tier 1-escalated cases.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="entry-points-for-different-personas"&gt;Entry points for different personas&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Different teams engage Luna in different ways, so the application exposes three entry points. These range from fully automated alert ingestion to interactive, on-demand investigation. Each entry point maps to a persona and how their work arrives:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tines SOAR&lt;/strong&gt; – Primary volume driver. Automated alert ingestion feeds cases directly into Tier 1.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Security, Risk, and ProdOps engineers&lt;/strong&gt; – Direct API access for ad-hoc investigation requests.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Claude Desktop extension&lt;/strong&gt; – Chat-based interface for on-call engineers. An engineer opens the extension, describes what they want investigated, and Luna executes a deep-dive with full tool access.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="memory-and-learning-how-luna-gets-smarter-over-time"&gt;Memory and learning: How Luna gets smarter over time&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The memory layer is built on AgentCore memory. This layer is what separates Luna from a stateless triage tool. It supports pattern recognition, deduplication, and continuous accuracy improvement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="episodic-memory-30-day-rolling-window"&gt;Episodic memory (30-day rolling window)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Stores recent events and resolution outcomes. When a new alert arrives, Luna checks: “Have I seen this pattern in the last 30 days? What was the verdict? Is this a known behavior that I should allowlist?”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Concrete example:&lt;/strong&gt; A Greenlight infrastructure role generates a specific access pattern every Tuesday at 3 AM for scheduled maintenance. Without memory, Luna would flag this every week. With episodic memory, Luna learned after the first resolution that this pattern is expected and auto-allowlists it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="long-term-memory"&gt;Long-term memory&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Persists semantic preferences and resolution templates beyond the 30-day window:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;“This access pattern is expected because that’s the role’s purpose.”&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Greenlight’s historical case data shows “Cards from this specific dark web source have a 73 percent false-positive rate – prioritize deduplication.”&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Resolution templates for recurring case types that encode institutional knowledge.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="case-learnings-pipeline"&gt;Case-learnings pipeline&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A dedicated pipeline feeds resolution outcomes back into the system:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ingestion&lt;/strong&gt; – An AWS Lambda function captures every case resolution from Tines events.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Learnings + Dedup&lt;/strong&gt; – DynamoDB stores resolution patterns and deduplicates similar cases. When Luna resolves a case the same way three times, it records that as a confirmed pattern.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Digest&lt;/strong&gt; – A Lambda function processes new learnings into actionable context that supervisors consume at inference time.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Feedback loop&lt;/strong&gt; – Resolution learnings feed back into Tier 1, improving first-pass accuracy incrementally.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This pipeline drove Luna’s autonomous resolution rate from 83 percent (Tier 1 only) to more than 90 percent (Tier 1, Tier 2, and learnings).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="expanding-from-soc-into-financial-risk-triage"&gt;Expanding from SOC into financial risk triage&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Greenlight expanded Luna into financial risk assessment within two months of the SOC deployment. The architectural changes were minimal: new specialized agents and a risk-domain supervisor layered onto the existing infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="dark-web-breach-card-workflow"&gt;Dark web breach card workflow&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a Greenlight customer card appears on the dark web, Luna executes this workflow autonomously:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ingest&lt;/strong&gt; – Threat intel providers (Mastercard, internal feeds) report compromised card numbers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Verify&lt;/strong&gt; – Luna queries Galileo: Does this card belong to Greenlight? Is it currently active? What’s the recent transaction history?&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deduplicate&lt;/strong&gt; – DynamoDB check: Is this a repeat notification? Has this card already been remediated? Multiple providers often report the same breach.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cross-reference&lt;/strong&gt; – Query Mixpanel for customer session anomalies, Zendesk for related tickets, and internal fraud scoring.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Assess and act&lt;/strong&gt; – Determine risk levels. Low risk: log and monitor. Medium risk: flag for review. High risk: escalate to human analysts with full evidence chain.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Greenlight receives hundreds of breach card notifications daily. Luna processes each in seconds. A human analyst doing the same work, logging into Galileo, checking DynamoDB, opening Mixpanel, searching Zendesk, takes 15-30 minutes per card.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table tracks Greenlight’s security operations on the metrics that matter most to the SOC. It compares three stages: manual triage before Luna, Tier 1 automation, and the full Tier 1 and Tier 2 system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Before Luna&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;With Tier 1&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;With Tier 1 and Tier 2&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Autonomous resolution rate&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0% (all manual)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;83%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;More than 90%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cases processed per month&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,000-4,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,000-4,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,000-4,000&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Median time-to-triage (routine)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;15-30 minutes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Seconds&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Seconds&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Human analyst escalations&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;100% of cases&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;17%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&amp;lt;10%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cost avoidance&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Baseline&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Significant&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Equivalent of multiple full-time employees&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Risk reduction&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Baseline&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Faster detection&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Consistent 24/7 quality&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Estimated analyst time avoided:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Based on Greenlight’s case volume and manual handling-time measurements, processing 4,000 cases at 15–30 minutes each would require approximately 1,000–2,000 analyst hours per month. Greenlight reports that Luna autonomously resolves more than 90 percent of these cases, avoiding an estimated 900–1,800 hours of routine manual work each month. Analysts can focus instead on cases that require human judgment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The risk reduction comes from two factors: speed (seconds compared to minutes reduces the exposure window) and consistency (Luna applies the same triage logic at 3 AM on Saturday that it does at 10 AM on Tuesday, with no fatigue and no shortcuts).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="five-lessons-from-building-luna"&gt;Five lessons from building Luna&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Building Luna and expanding it from security operations into financial risk surfaced five lessons for teams building multi-agent systems in regulated environments:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="start-with-a-bounded-domain-then-expand-horizontally"&gt;1. Start with a bounded domain, then expand horizontally&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Greenlight started with security triage because the success criteria were clear: resolve the case correctly or escalate. After the pattern proved reliable at 83 percent resolution, expanding financial risk required adding new specialized agents and a risk-domain supervisor, not rebuilding the architecture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="mirror-your-human-team-structure"&gt;2. Mirror your human team structure&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Tier 1/Tier 2 pattern maps directly to how SOC teams already operate. L1 analysts handle volume with playbooks. L2 analysts handle the complex cases with investigation skills. This made trust-building with the security team faster. They understood the model intuitively because it matched their own escalation logic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="memory-compounds-in-value"&gt;3. Memory compounds in value&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The episodic and long-term memory layer is what moved Luna from 83 percent to more than 90 percent. Each resolved case teaches Luna something: a new allowlisted pattern, a confirmed false positive source, a resolution template. After 6 months of operation, Luna has accumulated institutional knowledge that would take a new human analyst months to build.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="triage-first-action-later"&gt;4. Triage first, action later&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In a regulated environment, Greenlight designed Luna to triage and recommend, not to take autonomous remediation actions without explicit policy approval. The risk expansion follows the same principle. Luna provides evidence supporting its recommendation. In this human-in-the-loop model, a human analyst or an approved automated policy authorizes and executes remediation. This separation satisfied Greenlight’s compliance requirements and built trust with the risk team.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="share-specialized-agents-across-domains"&gt;5. Share specialized agents across domains&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When Greenlight expanded into financial risk, they reused existing specialized agents (Datadog, Zendesk, Mixpanel) and added domain-specific ones (Galileo, fraud scoring). The same agent that checks Zendesk tickets for security cases also checks them for risk cases. This reduces maintenance overhead and helps keep consistency across domains.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="infrastructure-stack"&gt;Infrastructure stack&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Luna runs entirely on managed AWS services. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is the orchestration runtime, and the rest of the stack handles scheduling, messaging, state, secrets, and integration. The following table lists each service and its role in the infrastructure:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Service&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Role&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Multi-agent orchestration runtime. VPC deployment, private subnets, direct code through ZIP and S3&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon EventBridge&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Scheduled rate-based triggers for Tier 2 deep-dive triage cycles&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon SNS&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Queue completions and notification routing between tiers&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Learnings storage, case deduplication, resolution pattern persistence&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon S3&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent packages and Terraform state management&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon CloudWatch Logs&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;30-day retention for audit trails and compliance evidence&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Secrets Manager&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;External API credentials&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Ingestion, resolution post-processing, kickoff, and digest functions&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon API Gateway&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Luna Queue API and MCP endpoint for external integrations&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-greenlight-is-building-next"&gt;What Greenlight is building next&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Building on the security and risk foundation, Greenlight is extending Luna’s multi-agent pattern into new operational domains and tooling:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Full risk domain coverage&lt;/strong&gt; – Autonomous triage for financial and business risk cases (in progress, launched about one month ago).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Product operations&lt;/strong&gt; – Exploring AWS DevOps Agent for incident investigation, root-cause analysis, and mitigation guidance. Greenlight is also evaluating how Luna’s existing agents could support operational investigations where their capabilities overlap.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Custom operational workflows&lt;/strong&gt; – Exploring AWS DevOps Agent custom agents, skills, and Model Context Protocol integrations for repeatable operational workflows and access to Greenlight’s internal tools.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS DevOps Agent and skills&lt;/strong&gt; – Combining the UI and skills framework of AWS DevOps Agent for script execution workflows (90-second execution time limit).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Well-Architected reviews&lt;/strong&gt; – Exploring Model Context Protocol servers for automated infrastructure assessments against AWS best practices.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;According to Greenlight’s production measurements, Luna autonomously resolves more than 90 percent of the company’s security and risk cases and processes up to 4,000 cases each month. Cases requiring additional judgment are escalated to human analysts. This human-in-the-loop approach allows Greenlight to automate routine triage while retaining control over remediation decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Teams in other regulated industries can apply the same approach by starting with a bounded use case, defining clear escalation criteria, and adding specialized agents and memory as the system matures. After the initial pattern proves reliable, teams can extend it to related operational domains.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, visit the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore product page&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;

  &lt;h3 class="lb-h4"&gt;Mani Teja Nissankararao&lt;/h3&gt; 
  &lt;p style="overflow: hidden"&gt;Mani Nissankararao is a Technical Account Manager at AWS who supports Financial Services Industry customers. He helps banks and fintechs run securely and resiliently on AWS, optimize costs, and adopt generative AI within regulatory guardrails. He partners with engineering and security teams on cloud migrations, operational excellence, and taking new AWS capabilities from proof of concept to production.&lt;/p&gt;
