		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">5ba6fab2d05a0a1835c7f5d2cacc32e13156d4b9</guid>

					<description>Gen Digital built Norton Revamp, a generative AI-powered professional reputation management platform, with an AI-curated News Engine grounded by Web Search on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. See how the Gen team delivers real-time, cited content suggestions, goal setting, and news feeds with zero data egress and Norton-grade privacy.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Your professional reputation now lives online first. Recruiters check LinkedIn before returning a call. Clients review your digital presence before booking a first meeting. For ambitious professionals, that shift is an opportunity: a well-crafted online voice can open doors that a résumé alone never could.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;But standing out takes more than content generation. It takes real-time awareness of what’s happening right now. A professional building a reputation as a generative AI thought leader needs to react to today’s breakthroughs, not last week’s headlines. And with feeds flooded by AI-generated noise, audiences reward authenticity and timeliness more than ever.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Web Search on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/em&gt; made that possible. We built &lt;a href="https://revamp.norton.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;em&gt;Norton Revamp&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, a generative AI-powered professional reputation management platform. At its heart is an AI-curated News Engine, powered by AgentCore Web Search, that grounds every content suggestion, goal recommendation, and news feed item in current, cited web knowledge. The platform launched in March 2026, built by a small group of engineers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show you how the &lt;a href="https://www.gendigital.com/us/en/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Gen&lt;/a&gt; team builds an AI-curated News Engine in Norton Revamp with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; Web Search to help customers turn ideas into engaging social media posts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-real-time-grounding-for-professional-content"&gt;The challenge: Real-time grounding for professional content&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Unlike chatbots that rely on static knowledge bases, Norton Revamp needs to understand what’s trending &lt;em&gt;right now&lt;/em&gt; in each user’s professional domain. A cyber security expert needs today’s threat intelligence. A marketing leader needs this week’s industry shifts. Traditional approaches (custom web crawlers, third-party news APIs, static content databases) each carry trade-offs: infrastructure overhead, stale data, vendor complexity, or data egress risk.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We evaluated three alternatives before choosing AgentCore Web Search. Custom crawlers require ongoing maintenance and lag real time. Third-party news APIs added vendor contracts, API key management, and data leaving our AWS environment. Static knowledge bases were perpetually stale. AgentCore Web Search eliminated these concerns. It’s fully managed with zero infrastructure overhead, operates entirely within AWS with zero data egress, and returns cited sources with URLs and publication dates. This makes personalized, real-time news feeds viable at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-agentcore-web-search-powers-norton-revamp"&gt;How AgentCore Web Search powers Norton Revamp&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Web Search on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is a fully managed web search capability, compliant with the Model Context Protocol (MCP). It’s available as a managed connector on the AgentCore Gateway, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Our agent sends a natural-language query, and Web Search returns relevant snippets, source URLs, titles, and publication dates that the model uses to produce grounded responses.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This capability powers three core features:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AI-curated news feed&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every user gets a personalized content stream surfacing the latest articles and trending topics tied to their goal. If you’re building a reputation as a generative AI thought leader, your feed prioritizes AI breakthroughs, industry analysis, and emerging use cases, sourced in real time through AgentCore Web Search. Unlike a static RSS aggregator, the feed is a goal-aware discovery engine that refreshes with every session.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Smart content suggestions&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Users receive AI-generated content ideas tailored to trending topics in their space, written in their own authentic voice, with optional AI-generated visuals. AgentCore Web Search grounds every suggestion on what’s happening &lt;em&gt;today&lt;/em&gt;. A three-step coaching flow (Analyze, Refine, Post) walks users from initial ideas to published content on LinkedIn and X, with a feedback loop, so the AI gets sharper with every iteration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trend-aware goal setting&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Users define a goal. The AI builds a personalized roadmap with weekly themes and milestones. AgentCore Web Search feeds real-time trend data into goal generation, so weekly themes reflect current industry movements. The system learns from the past four weeks of activity, refreshing recommendations as users grow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture-and-tech-stack"&gt;Architecture and tech stack&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 560px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/c-ml-22210-arch-diagram.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/29/c-ml-22210-arch-diagram.png" alt="Event-driven News Engine architecture showing Amazon Nova, AgentCore Web Search, and the AI Learning System grounding content generation" width="550"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Event-driven architecture of the Norton Revamp News Engine&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The integration follows an event-driven architecture. When a user sets or updates their professional goal, Amazon Nova (through Amazon Bedrock) generates targeted search queries based on the goal, recent activity, and engagement patterns. The agent sends these queries to AgentCore Web Search, which returns cited results entirely within our AWS environment. Our AI Learning System ranks the results for relevance, recency, and source credibility. The ranked results then serve as grounding context for Amazon Nova Lite to generate content suggestions in the user’s authentic voice. Engagement data feeds back into query generation, making the News Engine progressively smarter.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Generative AI and machine learning stack:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/strong&gt; – Core generative AI engine for content generation, personalization, post analysis (batch mode), and content coaching.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Nova (Nova Lite)&lt;/strong&gt; – Primary large language model (LLM) powering content generation, goal creation, and trend analysis.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Web Search on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/strong&gt; – Real-time web retrieval powering the News Engine, content grounding, and trend discovery through an MCP-compliant managed connector.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Stability AI&lt;/strong&gt; – Image generation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Claude (through Amazon Bedrock)&lt;/strong&gt; – Additional LLM capabilities for complex reasoning tasks.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Infrastructure:&lt;/strong&gt; React, TypeScript, and Tailwind CSS on the frontend. Node.js, Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS), AWS Lambda, Amazon DynamoDB (multi-table with composite key patterns), Amazon API Gateway, Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS), Amazon EventBridge, Amazon CloudFront, Amazon CloudWatch, and AWS Secrets Manager on the backend. Infrastructure managed through Terraform with blue-green deployment for zero-downtime releases.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Processing pipeline:&lt;/strong&gt; The platform runs four pipeline stages:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Full Scan&lt;/strong&gt; – Pull up to 1,000 historical posts, store in Amazon S3, run Amazon Bedrock batch analysis for reputation impact.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Daily Scan&lt;/strong&gt; – Amazon EventBridge-triggered incremental analysis.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Weekly Goal Generation&lt;/strong&gt; – Lambda generates goals considering past engagement data plus real-time trend data from AgentCore Web Search.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Content Coaching&lt;/strong&gt; – Synchronous Amazon Bedrock calls through the Analyze, Refine, Post flow, each step grounded with AgentCore Web Search results.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="engineering-with-an-ai-native-development-mindset"&gt;Engineering with an AI-native development mindset&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We embedded generative AI from day one, as both the product and the development methodology. The human-AI responsibility split was deliberate: human-owned architecture decisions, security reviews, business logic, and personalization algorithms. AI handled code generation, documentation, test scaffolding, refactoring, and UI component creation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With Claude, we produced 10 or more design variants per concept, compressed design-to-prototype time from weeks to hours and generated 70 percent of UI code with AI. We tested five pricing models in the time previously needed for one. The gamification system went through eight iterations before launching.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The measurable impact: 3x faster feature implementation, boilerplate generation accelerated by 90 percent, documentation by 80 percent, test creation by 70 percent, and UI component generation by 65 percent. The trade-off: AI output review consumed 20–30 percent of our time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AI helped with personalization algorithms, the DynamoDB multi-table architecture (which needed experience-based judgment), security implementation, and Amazon Bedrock batch processing optimization. The key insight: AI accelerated implementation, but humans designed how the AI Learning System should adapt to user goals. Product generative AI (Amazon Bedrock, Amazon Nova, AgentCore Web Search) and development generative AI served different purposes, and we kept them separate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="testing-security-and-quality-assurance"&gt;Testing, security, and quality assurance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We achieved 85 percent code coverage through automated test generation. Critical test areas included content generation quality, AgentCore Web Search result relevance and freshness validation, engagement tracking accuracy, gamification logic, and news feed relevance to user goals.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a product carrying the Norton brand, compliance is non-negotiable. Security checks spanned static code analysis, dependency vulnerability scanning, authentication flows, user data encryption, access control logic, generative AI model output validation, and content filtering. Our security team reviewed all AI-generated recommendations: AI accelerated identification, and humans made final decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The result: 99.9 percent uptime since launch with real-time monitoring of generative AI content quality and user satisfaction.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="best-practices-for-agentcore-web-search-integration"&gt;Best practices for AgentCore Web Search integration&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For teams considering AgentCore Web Search for real-time grounding:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Invest in query generation –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Well-structured queries yield better results than open-ended ones, so we focused on our prompt engineering there.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cache strategically –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Real-time freshness is the point, but some queries don’t need to re-execute every session. We cache results with time-to-live (TTL) values that match freshness requirements.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Build feedback loops –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Track which web search results users engage with and feed that signal back into query generation. Our News Engine’s relevance improved over the first 90 days.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Validate source quality –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;AgentCore Web Search returns source URLs and publication dates. We built a lightweight credibility scoring layer to prioritize authoritative sources.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Building Norton Revamp proved that a small, generative AI-augmented team can deliver enterprise software in months. It also proved that Web Search on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore can serve as the real-time intelligence backbone for products that need to stay current with the world.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We launched a generative AI-native professional reputation management platform in months, with personalized goal setting, AI-driven content suggestions, real-time curated news feeds grounded by AgentCore Web Search, adaptive learning, gamification, and Norton-grade security. A small group of engineers achieved 99.9 percent uptime.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Generative AI doesn’t replace engineers. It amplifies them. Pairing that amplification with fully managed services like AgentCore Web Search removes entire categories of infrastructure complexity. Humans designed the vision. Generative AI through Amazon Bedrock, Amazon Nova, and AgentCore Web Search accelerated bringing it to life, grounded in real-time, cited web knowledge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Web Search on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore eliminates the complexity of building and maintaining our own web retrieval infrastructure. It lets our &lt;a href="https://revamp.norton.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Norton Revamp&lt;/a&gt; News Engine deliver real-time, personalized content grounded in what’s happening in each user’s professional domain, with zero data egress and Norton-grade privacy. We went from concept to production in months, and AgentCore Web Search was a key enabler.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about Web Search on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, visit the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt;. Check out &lt;a href="https://revamp.norton.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Norton Revamp&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Build a semantic layer for agentic AI with EDB Postgres AI and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-semantic-layer-for-agentic-ai-with-edb-postgres-ai-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rajesh Nagpal]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 03 Nov 2026 16:41:13 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">070b0dd86152d3c36e4ca952542e5bc8e5351104</guid>

					<description>Learn how to build a governed semantic layer for agentic AI inside EDB Postgres AI and query it from a Strands Agents agent on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Business meaning, join paths, and access control all live in Postgres, with GRANT and row-level security enforcing what each agent can read.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show how to build a semantic layer for agentic AI using &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EDB Postgres AI&lt;/a&gt;. We run a &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents&lt;/a&gt; agent on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; that queries the layer to answer customer 360 (C360) questions. Earlier posts in this series built the same C360 pattern with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-semantic-layer-for-agentic-ai-on-aws-with-stardog-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Stardog’s knowledge graph&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-semantic-layer-for-agentic-ai-on-aws-with-denodo-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Denodo’s data virtualization platform&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-semantic-layer-for-agentic-ai-on-aws-with-atscale-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AtScale’s dimensional model&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-semantic-layer-for-agentic-ai-on-aws-with-strategy-mosaic-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strategy Mosaic’s linked models&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;EDB Postgres AI puts the semantic layer inside the operational Postgres database as an &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/overview/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AIDB extension&lt;/a&gt;. Business meaning lives next to the data in standard Postgres &lt;code&gt;COMMENT&lt;/code&gt; statements, the join graph comes from Postgres foreign keys, and governance uses Postgres &lt;code&gt;GRANT&lt;/code&gt; and row-level security through the &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/mcp-endpoint/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol (MCP) endpoint&lt;/a&gt; that ships with AIDB.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By the end of this post you will know:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;How to build an AIDB semantic knowledge base (SemKB) over a Postgres schema, how &lt;code&gt;COMMENT ON&lt;/code&gt; statements and &lt;code&gt;aidb.create_semantic_alias&lt;/code&gt; behave as the authoring surface, and how foreign keys crawl into join paths.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;How to expose the AIDB tool catalog to an external agent through the built-in EDB MCP endpoint, fronted by an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/elasticloadbalancing/latest/application/introduction.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Application Load Balancer (ALB)&lt;/a&gt; and registered as an MCP target on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-targets-mcp.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;How Postgres roles, column-level &lt;code&gt;GRANT&lt;/code&gt; statements, and row-level security produce different results for different callers. The prompt, tool catalog, and agent code stay the same.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;How AIDB captures &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/reference/observability/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenTelemetry traces&lt;/a&gt; for every SQL execution as rows in the &lt;code&gt;aidb_otel&lt;/code&gt; schema, queryable with ordinary SQL.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="three-layers-an-agent-needs"&gt;Three layers an agent needs&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An agent that answers business questions from live data needs three layers: a model that plans and writes queries, a meaning layer that tells the agent what the data represents, and a runtime that hosts the agent and controls identity and access. The &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-semantic-layer-for-agentic-ai-on-aws-with-stardog-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Stardog post&lt;/a&gt; in this series introduced this framing. The model layer in this post is &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-supported.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Anthropic Claude on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, the same as prior posts (model availability varies by &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Region&lt;/a&gt;). The runtime layer is &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agents-tools-runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore runtime&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-core-concepts.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt; handles inbound authorization, and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, holds outbound credentials for the tool targets, same as prior posts. EDB Postgres AI hosts agents and tools inside the database itself and acts as the connection point for external agents through its MCP endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The meaning layer is where this post diverges from the others. Here it is a &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/semantic-knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SemKB&lt;/a&gt; that lives inside the operational Postgres database. The schema and the business definitions are indexed together. Postgres &lt;code&gt;GRANT&lt;/code&gt;, row-level security, and column-level grants enforce access, not an advisory system prompt. The sections that follow cover how AIDB builds the layer, how AgentCore exposes it, and how two Postgres roles asking the same question get different answers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="example-use-case-c360-on-edb-postgres-ai"&gt;Example use case: C360 on EDB Postgres AI&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The dataset is the &lt;a href="https://github.com/stardog-union/knowledge-kits/tree/main/examples/c360" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Stardog C360 Knowledge Kit&lt;/a&gt;, loaded into a single EDB Postgres AI instance in a dedicated &lt;code&gt;c360&lt;/code&gt; schema. The data model has four tables and one derived view.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Object&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Key columns&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;customers&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;cid&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;first_name&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;last_name&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;full_name&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;email&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ssn&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;phone&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;picture&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;location&lt;/code&gt; (FK to &lt;code&gt;locations.id&lt;/code&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;locations&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;id&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;city&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;state&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;zip&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;products&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;id&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;name&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;brand&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;dept&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;price&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;purchases&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;id&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;cid&lt;/code&gt; (FK to &lt;code&gt;customers.cid&lt;/code&gt;), &lt;code&gt;pid&lt;/code&gt; (FK to &lt;code&gt;products.id&lt;/code&gt;), &lt;code&gt;date&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;quantity&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;price&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;big_spender&lt;/code&gt; (view)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;cid&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;full_name&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;state&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;large_orders&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;largest_order&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The physical schema is a plain Postgres join graph: a customer in the &lt;code&gt;customers&lt;/code&gt; table lives at a &lt;code&gt;location&lt;/code&gt;, places &lt;code&gt;purchases&lt;/code&gt;, and each purchase references a product. The semantic work rests on three business ideas that column names don’t reveal.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;State isn’t on the customer row. It lives in &lt;code&gt;locations&lt;/code&gt;, reached through the &lt;code&gt;customers.location&lt;/code&gt; foreign key, and a comment on that column tells SemKB where to find it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;purchases.price&lt;/code&gt; records the order total. An agent that reads it as a unit price and multiplies by &lt;code&gt;quantity&lt;/code&gt; returns $502,529 for the top customer instead of $27,991.66.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Big spender&lt;/em&gt; is a term the business owns. Captured as a view with a &lt;code&gt;COMMENT ON VIEW&lt;/code&gt; statement, its definition stays in one place that every agent, dashboard, and query reads from.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The questions we run through the agent end to end:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Who are our top 10 customers by total spend, and what state is each in?&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;How many big spenders do we have in each state?&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Can you list 10 customers and the state each lives in?&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Which 10 products have sold the most units?&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Can you list five customers with their Social Security numbers? (used in the governance rerun).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="reference-architecture"&gt;Reference architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the request path from the analyst through Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to the semantic layer inside EDB Postgres AI. The analyst authenticates with Amazon Cognito and calls AgentCore Gateway with the issued JSON Web Token (JWT) as a &lt;code&gt;Bearer&lt;/code&gt; token. Gateway routes to AgentCore runtime, which plans with Claude on Amazon Bedrock. The agent calls MCP tools through Gateway, which forwards to an Application Load Balancer in the EDB Postgres AI virtual private cloud (VPC) and on to the &lt;code&gt;edb-endpoints&lt;/code&gt; MCP server inside AIDB. AIDB serves the SemKB, semantic aliases, and read-only PostgreSQL tables. AgentCore traces go to Amazon CloudWatch, and AIDB captures OpenTelemetry traces as rows in the &lt;code&gt;aidb_otel&lt;/code&gt; schema inside Postgres.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/28/ML-22159-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/28/ML-22159-1-1.png" alt="Request path from the analyst through Amazon Cognito and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to the EDB Postgres AI semantic layer behind an Application Load Balancer" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Reference architecture for EDB Postgres AI and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two flows cross the boundary between AgentCore and EDB Postgres AI.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Inbound:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;A client calls AgentCore Gateway with a bearer token issued by an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity-idp-cognito.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Cognito user pool&lt;/a&gt;. The Gateway &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/inbound-jwt-authorizer.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;inbound JWT authorizer&lt;/a&gt; validates the token against the Cognito discovery URL. Gateway then evaluates its &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy-authorization-flow.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cedar policy&lt;/a&gt; against the token’s claims to decide which tools this agent, acting for this user, can invoke. It then routes the call to AgentCore runtime. The agent invokes Claude on Amazon Bedrock for planning and answer composition.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Outbound to the semantic layer:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The agent’s tool call is an &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MCP&lt;/a&gt; request. AgentCore Gateway supports &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-targets-mcp.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MCP server targets&lt;/a&gt; directly. AIDB’s &lt;code&gt;edb-endpoints&lt;/code&gt; HTTP server speaks &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/mcp-endpoint/connecting-a-client/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;streamable HTTP MCP with HTTP Basic authentication&lt;/a&gt;, which lets Gateway treat it as an MCP server target with no translation layer. An Application Load Balancer with an HTTPS listener and an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/acm/latest/userguide/acm-overview.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Certificate Manager (ACM)&lt;/a&gt; certificate fronts &lt;code&gt;edb-endpoints&lt;/code&gt; on an Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) instance. The ALB URL is registered as the Gateway target. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity-outbound-credential-provider.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;The AgentCore Identity credential provider&lt;/a&gt; holds the Postgres role credential and injects it as Basic auth on every outbound request. The agent code doesn’t see the Postgres credential.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Observability:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;AgentCore emits traces for tool calls, large language model (LLM) calls, and token usage to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability-configure.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CloudWatch Transaction Search&lt;/a&gt;. AIDB captures &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/reference/observability/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenTelemetry traces for SQL execution&lt;/a&gt; as rows in the &lt;code&gt;aidb_otel&lt;/code&gt; schema inside Postgres, queryable with ordinary SQL after the database is configured to write them (see &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/agent-governance/latest/connecting_aidb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;enabling telemetry on the source database&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;No AWS Lambda is required in this architecture. AIDB already speaks MCP, so Gateway talks to it as an MCP server target directly through the ALB. The Denodo post in this series uses the same ALB pattern in front of its MCP server, and the architecture here is unchanged other than the vendor.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="build-the-semantic-layer"&gt;Build the semantic layer&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These steps assume &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AIDB is installed and enabled&lt;/a&gt; on the EDB Postgres AI instance and the Stardog C360 kit’s four CSVs are loaded into a dedicated &lt;code&gt;c360&lt;/code&gt; schema with the primary and foreign keys shown earlier. Because this layer lives in the same database as the data, there is no second system to deploy and no catalog to synchronize. The semantic layer is defined in SQL, inside the same database as the application that writes the rows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An AIDB &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/semantic-knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;semantic knowledge base&lt;/a&gt; indexes Postgres schema, not data. It embeds the definition of every table, view, and column (with the &lt;code&gt;COMMENT ON&lt;/code&gt; text alongside) into a vector store that an agent can search in natural language. Three authoring tasks help the agent find the right table and column by meaning: writing &lt;code&gt;COMMENT ON&lt;/code&gt; statements, defining business terms as views with their own comments, and registering recurring questions as &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/semantic-knowledge-bases/semantic-aliases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;semantic aliases&lt;/a&gt;. Comments are the simplest of the three authoring tasks and improve text-to-SQL accuracy the most for both users and agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="add-comments-where-the-agent-will-look"&gt;Add comments where the agent will look&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;COMMENT ON COLUMN c360.purchases.price IS
  'Total amount the customer paid for the entire order, in US dollars. Not the unit price.';
COMMENT ON COLUMN c360.purchases.cid IS
  'Customer who placed the order. Joins to customers.cid.';
COMMENT ON COLUMN c360.customers.location IS
  'Home address of the customer. Joins to locations.id. State is in locations.state.';
COMMENT ON COLUMN c360.customers.ssn IS
  'US Social Security number. Sensitive, column-level grant required.';

CREATE VIEW c360.big_spender AS
  SELECT c.cid, c.full_name, l.state,
         count(*)      AS large_orders,
         max(pu.price) AS largest_order
    FROM c360.purchases pu
    JOIN c360.customers c ON c.cid = pu.cid
    JOIN c360.locations l ON l.id  = c.location
   WHERE pu.price &amp;gt; 250
   GROUP BY c.cid, c.full_name, l.state;

COMMENT ON VIEW c360.big_spender IS
  'Big spenders. A big spender is a customer with at least one order over 250 US dollars. '
  'Use this view for any question about big spenders.';&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The view holds the business definition in one place, so no agent or dashboard has to redefine &lt;em&gt;big spender&lt;/em&gt;. The time you spend on comments up front pays off on every query the agent runs afterward.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="create-the-semantic-knowledge-base"&gt;Create the semantic knowledge base&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create the SemKB over the &lt;code&gt;c360&lt;/code&gt; schema. The call registers the name, the embedding model, the target schemas, and the auto-processing mode.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT aidb.create_semantic_kb(
    name            =&amp;gt; 'c360_kb',
    model           =&amp;gt; 'bge-small-en-v1.5-f16',
    schemas         =&amp;gt; ARRAY['c360'],
    auto_processing =&amp;gt; 'Live'
);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The embedding model is a local llama.cpp model (&lt;code&gt;llamacpp_embeddings&lt;/code&gt;) that ships with the AIDB Amazon Machine Image (AMI). SemKB uses one embedding model per KB, so every entry sits in the same vector space. The AIDB AMI ships with a default SemKB named &lt;code&gt;semkb&lt;/code&gt; over &lt;code&gt;public&lt;/code&gt;, so creating &lt;code&gt;c360_kb&lt;/code&gt; leaves two SemKBs on the instance. When more than one SemKB exists, every &lt;code&gt;aidb.semantic_kb_search&lt;/code&gt; call must pass &lt;code&gt;kb_name&lt;/code&gt; explicitly. To swap the local model for a remote embedding model, such as &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/titan-embedding-models.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Titan Text Embeddings on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, register the remote model with &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/integrating-models/remote-models/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;aidb.create_model&lt;/a&gt;. Pass its name as the SemKB’s &lt;code&gt;model&lt;/code&gt; parameter. The &lt;code&gt;auto_processing&lt;/code&gt; parameter accepts &lt;code&gt;Disabled&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Live&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;Background&lt;/code&gt;. &lt;code&gt;Live&lt;/code&gt; reacts to schema changes through Postgres event triggers. &lt;code&gt;Background&lt;/code&gt; processes them asynchronously from a worker. &lt;code&gt;Disabled&lt;/code&gt; waits for an explicit &lt;code&gt;aidb.refresh_semantic_kb()&lt;/code&gt;. SemKB also crawls &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/semantic-knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;foreign keys into relationships&lt;/a&gt; at the same time. The &lt;code&gt;purchases.cid&lt;/code&gt; foreign key becomes a join path the agent can discover by meaning, not by column name.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="capture-recurring-questions-as-semantic-aliases"&gt;Capture recurring questions as semantic aliases&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/semantic-knowledge-bases/semantic-aliases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;semantic alias&lt;/a&gt; is a named, parameterized read-only &lt;code&gt;SELECT&lt;/code&gt; with a natural-language description that SemKB indexes alongside the schema metadata. The agent finds it by meaning, calls it with parameters, and returns a governed answer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT aidb.create_semantic_alias(
    name        =&amp;gt; 'big_spenders_by_state',
    description =&amp;gt; 'Count big spenders grouped by state, ordered from most to fewest.',
    query_text  =&amp;gt; $$SELECT state, count(*) AS big_spenders
                     FROM c360.big_spender
                     GROUP BY state
                     ORDER BY big_spenders DESC, state$$,
    params      =&amp;gt; '[]'
);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Aliases are AIDB’s reusable text-to-SQL building block. They sit next to tables and views in the SemKB index and show up to an agent as a tool. For a full &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/semantic-knowledge-bases/text-to-sql/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;text-to-SQL walkthrough&lt;/a&gt;, see the &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/semantic-knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EDB documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="govern-access"&gt;Govern access&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Governance in this architecture runs through Postgres primitives all the way down. The AIDB MCP endpoint has no permission system of its own: “Postgres grants decide what each agent can run” (&lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/mcp-endpoint/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MCP endpoint reference&lt;/a&gt;). Each MCP request authenticates with a Postgres username and password over its own connection, so &lt;code&gt;current_user&lt;/code&gt; inside every tool call is the agent’s role, visible in &lt;code&gt;pg_stat_activity&lt;/code&gt; like other connections.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram traces how identity flows from the end user through AgentCore to the Postgres role that evaluates the SQL, and how the resulting trace is captured for audit. The chain runs in four phases: inbound trust at the Gateway, outbound credential from AgentCore Identity, enforcement in Postgres through GRANT and row-level security, and audit through OpenTelemetry traces in the &lt;code&gt;aidb_otel&lt;/code&gt; schema.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/28/ML-22159-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/28/ML-22159-2-1.png" alt="Identity flow from the end user’s Amazon Cognito token through AgentCore Gateway and Identity to the Postgres role that runs the SQL" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Identity propagation chain from the end user to the Postgres role that evaluates the SQL&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Inbound trust at Gateway:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;1. The end user authenticates with Amazon Cognito and receives a JWT bearer token. 2. The client calls AgentCore Gateway with the token in the &lt;code&gt;Authorization&lt;/code&gt; header. 3. The Gateway &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/inbound-jwt-authorizer.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;JWT authorizer&lt;/a&gt; validates the token against the Cognito user pool’s discovery URL. 4. Gateway evaluates its &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy-authorization-flow.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cedar policy&lt;/a&gt; against the token’s claims to decide which tool this agent can call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Outbound credential to AIDB:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;5. Gateway resolves the outbound credential for the MCP target from &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity-outbound-credential-provider.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;the AgentCore Identity credential provider&lt;/a&gt;. For AIDB, this is a Postgres username and password used as HTTP Basic auth. 6. Gateway forwards the MCP streamable-HTTP request to the ALB, which forwards it to &lt;code&gt;edb-endpoints&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enforcement in Postgres:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;7. &lt;code&gt;edb-endpoints&lt;/code&gt; opens a Postgres connection using the username and password from the Basic auth header. Postgres authenticates the login role per &lt;code&gt;pg_hba.conf&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;current_user&lt;/code&gt; inside the tool call is that role. 8. Column-level &lt;code&gt;GRANT&lt;/code&gt; statements and row-level security policies on &lt;code&gt;c360&lt;/code&gt; tables enforce what rows and columns the tool call returns. A denial surfaces as a Postgres permission-denied error on the restricted table or column, which the MCP endpoint forwards unchanged.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Audit.&lt;/strong&gt; 9. AIDB captures OpenTelemetry traces for the SQL execution as rows in the &lt;code&gt;aidb_otel&lt;/code&gt; schema, available through SQL immediately.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="two-postgres-roles-same-question-different-results"&gt;Two Postgres roles, same question, different results&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Define two agent login roles that differ only in a column-level &lt;code&gt;GRANT&lt;/code&gt;: &lt;code&gt;mcp_agent_full&lt;/code&gt; sees every column, &lt;code&gt;mcp_agent_restricted&lt;/code&gt; sees every column except &lt;code&gt;customers.ssn&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;CREATE ROLE mcp_agent_full       LOGIN PASSWORD '...';
CREATE ROLE mcp_agent_restricted LOGIN PASSWORD '...';

GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA c360 TO mcp_agent_full, mcp_agent_restricted;

-- Both roles need aidb_users to list MCP tools
GRANT aidb_users TO mcp_agent_full, mcp_agent_restricted;

-- Full role sees every column
GRANT SELECT ON ALL TABLES IN SCHEMA c360 TO mcp_agent_full;

-- Restricted role sees every table and view, with customers.ssn withheld
GRANT SELECT ON c360.locations, c360.products, c360.purchases, c360.big_spender
  TO mcp_agent_restricted;
GRANT SELECT (cid, first_name, last_name, email, phone, picture, location, full_name)
  ON c360.customers TO mcp_agent_restricted;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both login roles need password-auth entries in &lt;code&gt;pg_hba.conf&lt;/code&gt; for the local &lt;code&gt;edb-endpoints&lt;/code&gt; connection path, as covered in &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/mcp-endpoint/configuring-agent-roles/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;configuring your agent role&lt;/a&gt;. Running the same question through both roles produces different results. The agent calling under &lt;code&gt;mcp_agent_restricted&lt;/code&gt; can’t project the &lt;code&gt;ssn&lt;/code&gt; column: the moment it tries, Postgres returns &lt;code&gt;permission denied for column ssn&lt;/code&gt; and the MCP endpoint forwards that error unchanged. The agent then responds that it can’t read SSNs. The prompt, the tool catalog, the SemKB, and the agent code are identical between the two roles. The only difference is the credential AgentCore Identity injects on the outbound Basic auth header.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Row-level filtering follows the same pattern. Enable row-level security (RLS) on a target table and attach per-role &lt;code&gt;USING&lt;/code&gt; policies. The AIDB MCP endpoint sees the same rows the role would see in a direct &lt;code&gt;psql&lt;/code&gt; connection.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A note on AIDB purposes.&lt;/strong&gt; AIDB also ships &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/governance/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;purposes&lt;/a&gt;, a policy abstraction that confines in-database agents (those created with &lt;code&gt;aidb.create_agent&lt;/code&gt; and invoked with &lt;code&gt;aidb.agent_converse&lt;/code&gt;) to a named Postgres role. Purposes apply to the in-database agent path. The external AgentCore path this post uses governs through Postgres roles directly at the MCP endpoint, as described earlier. For audit of external MCP agents, see &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/agent-governance/latest/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EDB Agent Governance&lt;/a&gt;, which reads the same &lt;code&gt;aidb_otel&lt;/code&gt; spans and visualizes agent sessions and policy decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Responsible AI considerations.&lt;/strong&gt; The governance surface shown so far controls what the agent &lt;em&gt;can execute&lt;/em&gt; at the database layer. Pair it with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; on the model side to shape what the agent &lt;em&gt;is allowed to generate&lt;/em&gt;: content filters for sensitive topics, denied-topic lists, and PII redaction. The two layers complement each other. Postgres &lt;code&gt;GRANT&lt;/code&gt; stops the agent from reading SSNs at the data layer. Guardrails help prevent PII patterns from leaking through other paths in the model response. For agents answering business questions over customer data, both are production controls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="expose-the-layer-and-run-the-agent"&gt;Expose the layer and run the agent&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Enabling the AIDB MCP endpoint takes two Postgres parameters plus a restart. Set &lt;code&gt;edb.endpoints_mcp_enabled = on&lt;/code&gt; in &lt;code&gt;postgresql.conf&lt;/code&gt;, add &lt;code&gt;aidb&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;shared_preload_libraries&lt;/code&gt;, and restart (&lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/mcp-endpoint/enabling-the-endpoint/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;enabling the endpoint&lt;/a&gt;). To reach the endpoint from outside the EC2 instance, set &lt;code&gt;edb.endpoints_mcp_host&lt;/code&gt; to a non-loopback address such as &lt;code&gt;0.0.0.0&lt;/code&gt;. Choose a port with &lt;code&gt;edb.endpoints_mcp_port&lt;/code&gt; (default is 8765). Supply the Transport Layer Security (TLS) pair with &lt;code&gt;edb.endpoints_mcp_tls_cert&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;edb.endpoints_mcp_tls_key&lt;/code&gt;, which the endpoint requires for non-loopback binds (&lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/aidb/latest/mcp-endpoint/configuring-the-endpoint/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;configuring the endpoint&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An Application Load Balancer with an HTTPS listener and an ACM certificate exposes &lt;code&gt;edb-endpoints&lt;/code&gt; to AgentCore Gateway. The ALB target group uses HTTPS to the EC2 instance on port 8765 and doesn’t validate the backend certificate chain, so a self-signed certificate on &lt;code&gt;edb-endpoints&lt;/code&gt; is sufficient for development and testing. For production, use an internal ALB with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-vpc-egress.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway VPC egress&lt;/a&gt; to keep connectivity between Gateway and the ALB private.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create an AgentCore Gateway with a Cognito authorizer, register the ALB URL as an MCP server target, and store the two Postgres credentials in the AgentCore Identity credential provider (&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity-outbound-credential-provider.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;credential provider reference&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent is a short Python script. It uses &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents/sdk-python" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents&lt;/a&gt; to wire a &lt;code&gt;BedrockModel&lt;/code&gt; pointing at the latest Claude Sonnet to an &lt;code&gt;MCPClient&lt;/code&gt;. The &lt;code&gt;MCPClient&lt;/code&gt; connects to the Gateway URL over the &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;streamable HTTP MCP transport&lt;/a&gt; with the Cognito access token as a &lt;code&gt;Bearer&lt;/code&gt; header. The system prompt instructs the agent to call &lt;code&gt;semantic_kb_search&lt;/code&gt; first for schema context, passing &lt;code&gt;kb_name =&amp;gt; 'c360_kb'&lt;/code&gt;, then &lt;code&gt;execute_sql&lt;/code&gt; to run the generated query. The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime-get-started-toolkit.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore runtime starter toolkit with Strands&lt;/a&gt; has a worked example of hosting an agent of this shape in production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We recommend turning on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock prompt caching&lt;/a&gt; for the agent’s system prompt. The same instruction and the same tool list are reused on every call, which is what prompt caching is for.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="sample-queries-and-verification"&gt;Sample queries and verification&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Four questions through the agent plus one governance rerun, all against the C360 data loaded earlier.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="query-1-who-are-our-top-10-customers-by-total-spend-and-what-state-is-each-in"&gt;Query 1: Who are our top 10 customers by total spend, and what state is each in?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Without the comment on &lt;code&gt;purchases.price&lt;/code&gt;, an agent configured with only a SQL execution tool reads &lt;code&gt;price&lt;/code&gt; as a unit price and multiplies by &lt;code&gt;quantity&lt;/code&gt;. The top row comes back as roughly $502,529 for Carolyn Inworth, which is wrong. One column comment, indexed by SemKB, is the entire difference between that answer and the right one:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;     full_name     |     state      |   spent
-------------------+----------------+----------
 Carolyn Inworth   | Wisconsin      | 27991.66
 Marlane Shadwick  | Florida        | 22677.05
 Isaac Popping     | Pennsylvania   | 20085.96
 Bria McKellar     | Texas          | 19588.80
 Mike Laydel       | Louisiana      | 18651.28
 Fayette Mor       | Arizona        | 17993.69
 Reynard Perigoe   | Florida        | 17019.55
 Humfrey Fores     | California     | 16811.16
 Patrice Jakeway   | California     | 16656.72
 Mord Pettus       | North Carolina | 16604.37&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Same model, same prompt, same tool catalog. The semantic knowledge base changes the answer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="query-2-how-many-big-spenders-do-we-have-in-each-state"&gt;Query 2: How many big spenders do we have in each state?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent finds the &lt;code&gt;big_spender&lt;/code&gt; view through &lt;code&gt;semantic_kb_search&lt;/code&gt; because its &lt;code&gt;COMMENT ON VIEW&lt;/code&gt; matches the natural-language question. The business definition sits in exactly one place:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;        state         | big_spenders
----------------------+--------------
 Florida              |           46
 Texas                |           46
 California           |           42
 District of Columbia |           24
 New York             |           23
 ...&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the business later redefines a big spender as a customer with cumulative spend over $5,000, the change is one &lt;code&gt;CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW&lt;/code&gt; statement. Every agent sees the new definition the next time SemKB refreshes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="query-3-list-10-customers-and-the-state-each-lives-in"&gt;Query 3: List 10 customers and the state each lives in&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The state is in &lt;code&gt;locations&lt;/code&gt;, not &lt;code&gt;customers&lt;/code&gt;. The join path is encoded in the &lt;code&gt;customers.location&lt;/code&gt; foreign key and in the column comment, both indexed by SemKB. The agent emits:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT c.cid, c.full_name, l.state
  FROM c360.customers c
  JOIN c360.locations l ON l.id = c.location
 ORDER BY c.cid
 LIMIT 10;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt; cid |       full_name       |     state
-----+-----------------------+----------------
   1 | Jolynn Bithany        | Texas
   2 | Mord Pettus           | North Carolina
   3 | Lavinie Stannislawski | Florida
   4 | Kimball Evenett       | Alabama
   5 | Linnie Meatcher       | California
   6 | Lorens Stoneley       | Washington
   7 | Juliane Gerhartz      | California
   8 | Jacob O'Lyhane        | Utah
   9 | Terrel Seller         | Missouri
  10 | Jannel Gawthrope      | California&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="query-4-which-10-products-have-sold-the-most-units"&gt;Query 4: Which 10 products have sold the most units?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A plain aggregation that runs inside Postgres next to the data:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT p.name, sum(pu.quantity) AS units
  FROM c360.purchases pu
  JOIN c360.products  p  ON p.id = pu.pid
 GROUP BY p.id, p.name
 ORDER BY units DESC, p.id
 LIMIT 10;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;        name        | units
--------------------+-------
 Markers            |   160
 Cleaning Wipes     |   155
 Playset (Girl's)   |   146
 Queen Sheet Set    |   138
 Mechanical Pencils |   137
 Shot Glasses       |   133
 Parchment Paper    |   127
 American Cheese    |   127
 Pop Music          |   127
 Varnish            |   125&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="governance-rerun-list-five-customers-with-their-social-security-numbers"&gt;Governance rerun: List five customers with their Social Security numbers&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same prompt runs twice. Under &lt;code&gt;mcp_agent_full&lt;/code&gt;, the agent returns five customer rows with their &lt;code&gt;ssn&lt;/code&gt; values populated. Under &lt;code&gt;mcp_agent_restricted&lt;/code&gt;, the agent’s tool call fails with the Postgres error &lt;code&gt;permission denied for column ssn&lt;/code&gt;, and the agent responds with a short message that SSNs are not accessible to this caller. The failure is enforced by the column-level &lt;code&gt;GRANT&lt;/code&gt;, not by the prompt or the model. AIDB captures the OpenTelemetry trace for the SQL execution in &lt;code&gt;aidb_otel&lt;/code&gt;, so a reviewer can query the trace row to confirm the governance decision later.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="extending-across-data-sources"&gt;Extending across data sources&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For analytical workloads that outgrow the operational database, &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/pgaa/latest/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EDB Postgres Analytics Accelerator (PGAA)&lt;/a&gt; lets Postgres read Apache Iceberg, Delta Lake, and Parquet tables in object storage through an Iceberg REST catalog. The &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/docs/edb-postgres-ai/analytics/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EDB Postgres AI analytics overview&lt;/a&gt; covers the end-to-end setup. PGAA tables appear in Postgres as ordinary tables, so the same SemKB description keeps working against the analytical copy and the agent’s tool catalog doesn’t change.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For federation to external relational sources, Postgres &lt;a href="https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/ddl-foreign-data.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;foreign data wrappers&lt;/a&gt; make an external table reachable as if it were local. The semantic layer is unchanged in either case because business definitions, column comments, and semantic aliases live in Postgres.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deployment"&gt;Deployment&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two shapes work for this architecture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Self-managed on Amazon EC2.&lt;/strong&gt; Use the EDB Postgres AI Agent Factory AMI from the AWS Marketplace. The customer owns the VPC, the subnet, the version cadence, and the EDB Postgres AI software upgrades. The ALB and the AgentCore Gateway target configuration are unchanged from the preceding architecture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;EDB-managed on Amazon EC2.&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://www.enterprisedb.com/services/managed-database" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EDB Managed Database&lt;/a&gt; is a service where EDB experts handle database administration on the customer’s behalf. The AgentCore side is unchanged.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reliable agentic analytics needs three things working together: a foundation model that can plan and generate queries, a semantic layer that provides a governed business view of the data, and an agent runtime that hosts the agent and manages identity. Amazon Bedrock supplies the model, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore supplies the runtime, and EDB Postgres AI supplies a semantic layer that lives inside the operational Postgres database. Business meaning, join paths, and governance come from Postgres primitives already present in the schema. The AWS side is the same as the rest of this series: AgentCore Gateway, Runtime, Identity, Bedrock, and CloudWatch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The data, the business definitions, and the access rules all live in one Postgres schema, so there is no separate catalog to keep in sync. Audit lives in the same database: &lt;code&gt;aidb_otel&lt;/code&gt; holds a span per tool call, next to the data it ran against.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="where-to-start"&gt;Where to start&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To apply this architecture to your own data, work through these steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Pick one Postgres database your agents need to query live. Narrower is better.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create a dedicated schema for the agent’s data, with &lt;code&gt;GRANT USAGE&lt;/code&gt; scoped to a service role.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Write &lt;code&gt;COMMENT ON&lt;/code&gt; statements on columns and views that use business language. Capture business terms as views with a &lt;code&gt;COMMENT ON VIEW&lt;/code&gt; statement.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create a SemKB over the schema with &lt;code&gt;aidb.create_semantic_kb&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;auto_processing =&amp;gt; 'Live'&lt;/code&gt; (or &lt;code&gt;Background&lt;/code&gt; under high write volume).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Register recurring questions as semantic aliases.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enable the AIDB MCP endpoint, front it with an ALB with HTTPS and an ACM certificate, and register the ALB URL as an AgentCore Gateway MCP server target.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run three real questions through the agent, then rerun one under a role with reduced grants to confirm the enforcement surface works end to end.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt; &lt;/p&gt;
